# The Earth's Crust, Rocks / India Geological Structure — Mains-Focused Live Edition

This is the Mains edition of Geography Topic 02. It teaches the crust as a General Studies Paper I object: what it is made of, how its three rock families are produced and recycled, how it is folded, faulted and rebuilt through orogenic time, why the map of continents and ocean basins is a transient configuration rather than a fixed container, and how that whole apparatus produces the specific stratigraphic ladder that India stands on. Geography is a **named GS-I owner**, so this edition addresses the paper directly rather than through another subject's door.

**One topic, two halves, one file.** The world half and the India half are separately examined, but they are not separate topics and they are not split into separate files here. The classification frame built in the world half is the frame the Indian rock systems are sorted into; the orogenic chronology that dates the Caledonian and the Hercynian is the chronology that makes the Aravali intelligible as a relict; the collision that closes an ocean basin in the world half is the collision that converts the Tethys into the Himalaya in the India half. Every one of Lessons 1 to 8 therefore closes on an explicit India hook, Lesson 9 is a bridge that belongs to both, and Lessons 10 to 14 carry India.

**The honest shape of this topic, and it is the inverse of Topic 01's.** Seven verified previous-year demands are owned. **Both Mains demands sit in the world half** — primary rocks and crustal tectonics, worth twenty-five marks between them — and the India half owns **no Mains demand at all**. Yet the India half cannot be taught thinly, for three reasons: the syllabus clause this topic directly owns is *world* physical geography, so the world half must be deep anyway and gains nothing from India being starved; an India-light Geography edition is non-compliant with the India-centric rule this folder works under; and the topic owns a live **2026** objective demand on the Peninsular Block's tectonic and geomorphic characteristics, which is in substance a compressed Mains question. **The world half therefore earns its depth from mark-value and the India half earns equal depth from conceptual load and chronological difficulty.** That is the central design decision of this file.

**A classification choice, taught as a choice.** India's rocks are arranged here on the fourfold **Archaean / Purana / Dravidian / Aryan** scheme associated with Holland and the Geological Survey of India, because the Advanced owner of this topic locks that scheme. A second respected Indian source arranges the same rocks differently, placing the Aravalli, Dharwar, Cuddapah and Vindhyan sequences all inside an "Archaean System". ❗ That divergence is **stated openly at Lesson 10 and is not silently harmonised**, because a candidate who knows that the ladder is a scheme rather than a fact writes a better paragraph than one who recites it.

**Answer-neutrality.** Objective demands are displayed with their year, paper, question number and conceptual demand only. No answer letter, truth marking, elimination cue or corrective mapping appears anywhere in this file — including for the two demands whose official Set-A keys are physically held in the repository, and including for the 2026 demand whose key is provisional. What is taught is the *mechanism* each question tests, because that is what a Mains answer can use. No solved previous-year model answers appear; every model answer here is **original** and is marked as such.

**What is deliberately left out.** Era-period-epoch time-scale tables, exhaustive mineral-occurrence district inventories, book-era production and reserve tables, volcano and earthquake case files, Himalayan physiographic sub-division detail, compiled four-option objective corpora and mineral crystallography. Each is either low-yield memorisation, stale data or another topic's property. Nothing that is a definition, a causal step, an objection, a reply, a qualification or a practice item has been removed.

## Roadmap

Fifteen lessons. The count is not a quota; it is what four independent inputs produce. **One**, a directly owned Mains clause and a directly owned Prelims clause, each split across two halves that are examined separately. **Two**, a conceptual dependency chain of unusual length — crust and seismic evidence, rock families and the cycle, igneous classification and forms, sedimentary and metamorphic families with the chronology toolkit, folding and faulting and orogenic time, the drift claim, the mechanism that rescued it, plate tectonics and the ocean-basin cycle, isostasy and the shield hierarchy, India's three provinces, then four Indian rock systems in order, then structure-to-resource causation, then mapwork — in which collapsing any early link breaks a later derivation. **Three**, seven owned demands sitting on five mutually unrelated concepts. **Four**, a mapwork obligation far heavier than Topic 01's: two controlling base maps, eight single-system distribution overlays, a four-panel palaeogeographic sequence, a plate map, a thickness profile, a lineament sketch and a hill-range inventory.

## Lesson 1 — The crust in section: composition, discontinuities, and what seismic evidence can and cannot prove

Progress: 1 / 15  |  Stage: Foundation  |  Subtopic: What the crust is made of, where its boundaries are, and how those boundaries are known

The column on the left is what you draw. The box on the right is why you are entitled to draw it. ⚠️ Keep the two apart in your head, because the commonest failure in an interior answer is to present the column as an observation when it is an **inference from wave behaviour**.

### 1. What this lesson owns, and what it hands to other topics

✅ One sentence of provenance justifies the whole topic. The term **geomorphology** arose in the **United States Geological Survey in the 1880s**, possibly coined by **J.W. Powell and W.J. McGee** — that is, the systematic study of the Earth's surface forms and the materials beneath them is a deliberate nineteenth-century creation, not an ancient category. ⚠️ Its founding question is this lesson's question: *what is the outer shell made of, and how do we know?*

⚠️ This topic owns the crust as a **material object to be classified**: what it is made of, how thick it is, where its boundaries lie and how those boundaries are known. Three neighbouring claims are owned elsewhere and are named here once, then left alone.

- 🔁 **Why the Earth has a metallic core at all** — condensation and differentiation — belongs to Topic 01. This lesson *assumes* differentiation and does not re-derive it.
- 🔁 **The geodynamo and the magnetosphere** likewise belong to Topic 01. They reappear here only at Lesson 7, where a reversal record is read out of cooling basalt.
- 🔁 **Earthquakes as events, seismic zonation, seismic hazard, tsunami and eruption styles** belong to Topic 03. This lesson uses seismic waves **only as evidence**, which is a different use of the same physics.

❗ That restraint is itself examinable discipline. A fifteen-mark tectonics answer that drifts into seismic zonation has spent marks on a demand it was not asked.

### 2. Sial and sima: the oldest useful contrast, in its own units

✅ G.C. Leong divides the outer Earth into three shells. The **crust** has an upper part rich in **silica and alumina**, called **sial**, of density about **2.7**, granitic in character; and a lower, heavier part rich in **silica, iron and magnesium**, called **sima**, of density about **3.0**, basaltic in character. Beneath it lies the **mantle or mesosphere**, olivine-rich and about **1,800 miles** thick, and below that the **core or barysphere**, of radius about **2,160 miles**, composed of iron and nickel and therefore called **nife**, with a temperature estimated at about **3,500 °F**. ✅ The crust itself he gives as **three to four miles thick under the oceans and up to thirty miles under the continents**, and his Fig. 16 draws the lighter continental block effectively **floating on the denser sima**.

⚠️ **Normalisation you must apply, and apply openly.** Those figures are in imperial units and belong to a mid-century textbook. Quote them **as Leong's**, in his units, with the attribution attached — do not silently convert them and present the result as current geophysics. The conceptual content is what survives: a **lighter, thicker, granitic continental crust standing on a denser, thinner, basaltic substrate**. That single contrast is the premise of isostasy at Lesson 9 and of the shield-versus-ocean-floor asymmetry at Lesson 8.

✅ The same contrast appears independently in Majid Husain with metric values: **oceanic crust averaging about 5 km against continental crust of 30-40 km**. ⚠️ Two sources, two unit systems, one structural claim — that convergence is what makes the claim safe to assert.

### 3. The two discontinuities worth naming, and the one distinction worth more than both

✅ The **Mohorovicic discontinuity**, the crust-mantle boundary, lies at an average of about **35 km**. ✅ The **Gutenberg discontinuity**, the mantle-core boundary, lies at about **2,900 km**. ✅ The source section carries depth markers at **305, 700, 2,900, 5,150 and 6,371 km**, and the accompanying figure gives the **lithosphere as 75-125 km**, the **crust as 5-65 km** and the **asthenosphere as 250-700 km**.

⚠️ Now the distinction that earns more than either name. **Crust is a compositional unit; lithosphere is a mechanical one.** The crust is defined by what it is made of and ends at the Moho. The lithosphere is defined by how it behaves — rigid — and includes the crust *plus* the rigid uppermost mantle, so it extends well below the Moho. The **asthenosphere** below it is defined the same way, by behaviour: soft and mobile enough for the overlying rigid shell to move across it.

❗ This is not pedantry. **Plates are lithospheric, not crustal.** An answer that says "the crust is broken into plates" has described the wrong object, and at Lesson 8 that error propagates into every statement about what subducts and what does not.

### 4. The evidence chain, stated as a chain

✅ Three wave types do the work. **P-waves** are compressional, are the fastest and therefore the first to arrive, and travel through solids and liquids alike. **S-waves** are shear waves and **cannot propagate through a liquid at all**. **Surface waves** are slowest, confined near the surface, and are the destructive ones — 🔁 which is Topic 03's business, not this lesson's.

⚠️ The chain runs: *S-waves are absent beyond a certain angular distance from an earthquake, producing a **shadow zone*** → *a liquid shell must begin at the depth that produces that shadow* → *P-waves do continue but are sharply refracted at that same depth* → *a sharp refraction requires a sharp change in material properties, so the boundary is real and not gradual*. ✅ Majid draws **four** inferences from the wave data, not one: a **solid inner core**, a **liquid outer core**, a **soft asthenosphere** and a **rigid lithosphere**.

⚠️ **What the evidence cannot prove.** It fixes depths, states and the sharpness of boundaries. It does not fix **composition**; the identification of the core as iron-nickel rests on density, meteorite analogy and the magnetic argument rather than on wave arrivals. Say so in an answer and you have shown the limit of your own evidence, which is a marked quality.

### 5. 🇮🇳 India hook — the same Moho, at two very different depths

⚠️ The Moho is not a horizontal plane. Where the crust is thin, it is shallow; where the crust is thickened, it is deep. India contains the extremes of that statement within one country. Beneath the **Peninsular block** sits an ancient, long-denuded, essentially stable continental crust; beneath the **Himalaya** sits crust that has been **shortened and thickened by continent-continent collision**, so the crust-mantle boundary there lies far deeper than the average quoted above.

❗ **Caution, and it is absolute.** Do **not** attach a number to either Indian case. No crustal-thickness figure for the Peninsula or for the Himalaya is carried by any source available to this edition, and an invented one is exactly the kind of error an examiner can identify with certainty. The *structural* statement — thin and stable under the shield, thickened and deep under the collision orogen — is fully sufficient, and Lesson 9 converts it into the isostatic root argument that actually earns the mark.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it establishes | What it cannot establish |
|---|---|---|
| Density contrast (sial / sima) | That continental material is lighter and stands higher than oceanic material | Where any boundary actually lies |
| Seismic-wave behaviour | Depths, states and the sharpness of internal boundaries | The chemical identity of any shell |
| Thickness measurements | That crust is thin under oceans and thick under mountains | Why it is thick there — that is isostasy and orogeny, at L5 and L9 |

**Strongest objection.** The interior is Topic 01's and the waves are Topic 03's. What is left for Topic 02?

**Reply.** The **crust** is left, and the crust is this topic's entire subject matter. Topic 01 establishes that a layered planet with a metallic core exists; Topic 03 treats the waves as a hazard. Between them sits the question neither answers: *what is the outer shell made of, how thick is it, and how do we know where it ends?* Every later lesson in this file — rock families, orogeny, plate kinematics, India's shield — is a statement about that shell. Remove this lesson and the topic has no defined object.

⚠️ **Residual.** The depth figures quoted here are **averages drawn from one textbook section**, and crustal thickness varies continuously with setting. Treat them as orders of magnitude that establish a contrast, not as constants.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership) and the Prelims physical-geography clause.
- **Verified linkage:** no demand is owned by this lesson alone. It is the premise lesson for **2025 GS-I Q16** (crustal tectonics) and for the 2026 Peninsular Block demand resolved at Lesson 9.
- **Probable framing:** this material appears in a Mains answer as a *two-sentence foundation* — the lithosphere is rigid, the asthenosphere beneath it is mobile, therefore motion is possible — and almost never as the demand itself.
- **Traps:** calling plates "crustal" rather than lithospheric; presenting the layer table as an observation; importing seismic zonation; quoting Leong's imperial figures as current metric geophysics; attaching an invented crustal thickness to the Himalaya.
- **Answer use:** one sentence buys the whole premise — *"A rigid lithosphere of crust plus uppermost mantle rides on a mobile asthenosphere, which is what makes horizontal displacement of continental blocks physically possible."* Spend no more here.

Revision notes:

- **Geomorphology** as a term arose in the **US Geological Survey in the 1880s**, possibly coined by **J.W. Powell and W.J. McGee** — a deliberate nineteenth-century creation, not an ancient category.
- **Crust** = compositional unit, ends at the **Moho**, average about **35 km**; **lithosphere** = mechanical unit, **75-125 km**, crust **plus** rigid uppermost mantle.
- **Plates are lithospheric, not crustal.** Highest-value terminology point in the lesson.
- **Asthenosphere 250-700 km**: soft, mobile, the layer the rigid shell moves across.
- **Sial** — silica + alumina, density ~**2.7**, granitic, upper. **Sima** — silica + iron + magnesium, density ~**3.0**, basaltic, lower. (Leong, in his own units.)
- Leong's shells: crust **3-4 miles** oceanic to **30 miles** continental · mantle/**mesosphere** ~**1,800 miles**, olivine-rich · core/**barysphere** radius ~**2,160 miles**, iron + nickel = **nife**, ~**3,500 °F**. ⚠️ Mid-century textbook, imperial, attribute it.
- Metric corroboration: oceanic crust ~**5 km**, continental **30-40 km**.
- **Moho** = crust/mantle; **Gutenberg** = mantle/outer core at ~**2,900 km**. Section markers **305 | 700 | 2,900 | 5,150 | 6,371 km**.
- **P-waves** compressional, fastest, cross solids **and** liquids. **S-waves** shear, **cannot cross a liquid**. Surface waves slowest and destructive — 🔁 hazard belongs to Topic 03.
- **Shadow zone** → liquid shell → **four** inferences: solid inner core, liquid outer core, soft asthenosphere, rigid lithosphere.
- Sharp **refraction** of P-waves at 2,900 km proves the boundary is sharp, not gradual.
- Seismic evidence fixes **depth, state and sharpness**; it does **not** fix composition.
- 🇮🇳 Moho shallow and stable under the Peninsula, deep under the thickened Himalayan crust. ❗ **No number** for either.
- 🔁 Differentiation and the geodynamo are Topic 01's; eruption styles and seismic hazard are Topic 03's.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Earth's crust is divided into seven major plates which float on the molten mantle." Identify the three separate errors and give the corrected sentence.

**Model answer:** First, the moving units are **lithospheric**, not crustal — the lithosphere comprises the crust plus the rigid uppermost mantle, so plates extend well below the Moho. Second, the mantle is **not molten**; the asthenosphere is soft, mobile and capable of slow flow, but the seismic evidence that establishes a liquid shell applies to the **outer core** beyond about 2,900 km, not to the mantle. Third, "float" imports a buoyancy relation that belongs to isostasy — the lithosphere moves *across* the asthenosphere, and vertical buoyant adjustment is a separate phenomenon with its own two competing models. Corrected: *"The lithosphere, comprising the crust and the rigid uppermost mantle, is divided into seven major plates and several minor ones, which move horizontally across the soft, mobile asthenosphere beneath."*

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not reach for "molten" merely because the mantle is hot and capable of flow. Solids near their melting point deform slowly without being liquid, and the one shell the seismic record actually proves to be liquid is the outer core — which is why S-waves vanish there and nowhere above it.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Our picture of the Earth's interior is an inference from wave behaviour rather than a description of an observation." Examine, with reference to the crust and its boundaries. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim holds. No part of the interior below shallow drilling has been observed; the layered column is reconstructed from how seismic waves behave. P-waves are compressional and cross both solids and liquids; S-waves are shear waves and cannot cross a liquid. The systematic absence of S-waves beyond a certain distance defines a shadow zone, which requires a liquid shell beginning at about 2,900 km — the Gutenberg discontinuity — while the sharp refraction of P-waves at that same depth shows the boundary to be abrupt rather than gradational. The same reasoning fixes the Moho at an average of about 35 km and distinguishes a rigid lithosphere from a soft asthenosphere. The limit must be stated: wave behaviour fixes depth, state and sharpness, not chemistry. That the core is iron-nickel rests on density and analogy, not on arrivals — so the column is a strongly constrained inference, not a photograph.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for the P/S contrast stated with the liquid-barrier property; 2 for the shadow zone used as the *reasoning step* rather than named as a fact; 2 for refraction cited as evidence of a sharp boundary; 2 for the stated limit that composition is not fixed by wave data; 1 for the Moho and Gutenberg depths; 1 for the crust-versus-lithosphere distinction used correctly. Deduct 2 for describing the mantle as molten.

```text
  THE SIX CRITERIA ON WHICH ANY TWO ROCKS DIFFER (Leong):
```

The loop is closed and it has **no privileged starting point**. ⚠️ That is the first analytic claim of the lesson: calling igneous the "primary" rock is a statement about *genesis of material*, not a statement about sequence in time, because igneous rock is itself produced by the remelting of earlier rock.

### A. The frame before the families

Most candidates begin with the three families. Begin one step earlier, with the question *on what dimensions can two rocks differ at all?*

✅ Leong supplies six: rocks differ in **texture, structure, colour, permeability, mode of occurrence and degree of resistance to denudation**.

⚠️ Learn that list as a **scoring skeleton**, because a directive of the form "describe the characteristics and types of X rocks" is marked against exactly these dimensions. A script that writes "granite is hard and has large crystals" has used one dimension. A script that moves through texture, then colour and composition, then permeability, then mode of occurrence, then resistance to denudation has produced six marked statements from one rock. Neither canonical owner states this frame, and it is the cheapest structural upgrade available in the whole topic.

| Criterion | What it actually asks | Why an examiner rewards it |
|---|---|---|
| **Texture** | Crystal size and arrangement | It encodes **cooling rate**, so it is a genetic statement disguised as a descriptive one |
| **Structure** | Stratified, foliated, massive or jointed | It separates the three families at a glance in the field |
| **Colour** | Light or dark | It proxies composition — acid rocks are pale, basic rocks dark |
| **Permeability** | Whether water passes | It is the bridge to aquifers, soils and relief — the whole of L14 |
| **Mode of occurrence** | Batholith, sill, flow, bed, band | It is the map-scale expression of the rock |
| **Resistance to denudation** | Whether it stands up or wears down | It converts petrology into **landscape**, which is where the marks are |

### B. What a rock is, and why the definition matters

✅ A **rock is an aggregate of minerals**. ✅ The principal rock-forming minerals are the **feldspars, micas, amphiboles, pyroxenes, olivine, quartz, calcite, dolomite, the clays and gypsum**.

⚠️ Two consequences follow immediately and both are examinable. First, a rock **name** is a statement about an *assemblage*, not about a single substance; this is why "granite" covers a range of compositions and why the same parent can yield different metamorphic products. Second, because minerals have different hardness, solubility and cleavage, the **behaviour of a rock under weathering is a weighted average of its minerals** — which is why a quartz-rich rock resists and a feldspar-rich rock decays to clay.

### C. The three families, defined by process rather than by appearance

✅ **Igneous** rock forms from the cooling and solidification of molten material. ✅ Within it, **plutonic or intrusive** rock cools slowly at depth and therefore grows **large, easily recognised crystals** — granite is the type example — and is exposed only later by denudation; **volcanic or extrusive** rock pours out as lava and solidifies fast, so its crystals are **tiny**, and basalt forms lava sheets and plateaux. ✅ Majid classifies igneous rock by **texture and composition** — basalt, gabbro, andesite, diorite, rhyolite, granite — and by **mode of occurrence** into intrusive and extrusive. ✅ Igneous rock is the **chief original source of the inorganic mineral matter** from which sedimentary rock is later built, which is the precise sense in which it is "primary".

✅ **Sedimentary** rock is built from accumulated sediment derived from pre-existing rock and from organic matter, and it is **stratified** — laid in layers that vary from inches to many feet. ✅ Sediment is transported by streams, glaciers, wind or even animals before deposition and cementation.

✅ **Metamorphic** rock is pre-existing rock altered by change in temperature, pressure and the chemical action of fluids, with **both texture and mineral composition** altered. ✅ The standard pairs are **shale or mudstone → slate or schist**, **limestone → marble**, **sandstone → quartzite**, and **granite or other felsic parents → gneiss**.

> 🔑 **Trap, and it is the one most often set.** ❌ "Foliation and stratification are the same thing." They are not. **Stratification** is sedimentary layering produced by deposition. **Foliation** is a metamorphic alignment of minerals produced by directed pressure. A schist splits along its **foliation**, not along a bedding plane — and in the field that single difference tells you which family you are holding.

> 🔑 **Second trap, carried from the Core owner.** ❌ "Coal → graphite" is often written as a universal one-step rule. ⚠️ Graphite **may** form by metamorphism of carbon-rich material, and the pair is a legitimate example, but it is not a universal transformation. ❗ And do **not** extend the pair to diamond: one local source offers "diamond from carbon" as a metamorphic example, and that is not a statement this edition reproduces.

### D. Permeability, which is the family trait that does the most later work

✅ Primary porosity is generally **low** in crystalline metamorphic rock, but **joints, fractures and weathered zones supply secondary permeability** — and that secondary route, not the pore space of the fresh rock, is what matters for groundwater and for foundations.

⚠️ Hold that thought for three later lessons. At Lesson 3 it explains why a resistant, impermeable granite forms high ground. At Lesson 11 it explains why the Cuddapah limestones behave differently from the Vindhyan sandstones. At Lesson 14 it is the step that converts "the Peninsula is crystalline" into "the Peninsula has limited groundwater in the rock mass but useful storage in fractures", which is an argument rather than a label.

### E. 🇮🇳 India hook — one Indian exemplar for every box in the table

⚠️ The fastest way to make this classification Indian is to force every family to produce a named Indian case, and then to notice that the Indian cases are not evenly distributed in time.

| Family and sub-class | Indian exemplar | Which Indian system it belongs to |
|---|---|---|
| Igneous, extrusive, fissure-fed | **Deccan Trap basalt** | Aryan (L12) |
| Igneous, intrusive, plutonic | **Closepet granite**, Karnataka | Archaean / Dharwar terrain (L10) |
| Sedimentary, mechanically formed | **Vindhyan sandstone** | Purana (L11) |
| Sedimentary, continental and coal-bearing | **Gondwana coal measures** | Aryan (L12) |
| Metamorphic, high-grade gneiss | **Bengal Gneiss**, also called **Khondolite** in the Eastern Ghats | Archaean (L10) |
| Metamorphic, charnockitic | **Nilgiri Gneiss / Charnockite series** | Archaean (L10) |

⚠️ Read the right-hand column and you have already learnt something structural: **India's igneous and metamorphic rocks cluster at the two ends of its chronology** — an Archaean crystalline basement at the bottom and a Cretaceous-to-Eocene flood basalt near the top — while the long middle is sedimentary. That is not a coincidence; it is what a stable shield does, and Lesson 9 explains why.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

**Strongest objection.** The three-family classification is school geography. Why spend a lesson on it in a Mains file?

**Reply.** Because the 2022 demand is literally a classification demand — *characteristics and types of primary rocks* — and because the India half of this topic is nothing but a classification exercise carried out in time instead of in substance. ⚠️ A candidate who holds only the three names has a label set; a candidate who holds the **six criteria plus the closed cycle** has a generative structure that produces paragraphs on demand. The difference shows up in the third sentence of the answer.

⚠️ **Residual.** The parent of a metamorphic rock is not always recoverable. For quartzite and marble it is obvious; for schist and gneiss it is often genuinely difficult, and the Core owner says so. An answer that claims every metamorphic rock announces its protolith has overstated the discipline.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** supports **2022 GS-I Q4** (characteristics and types of primary rocks, *Describe*, 10 marks), whose owning lesson is Lesson 3.
- **Probable framing:** as the opening classification paragraph of any rock, soil or mineral answer, and as the organising frame of a "rock type and human activity" question.
- **Traps:** equating foliation with stratification; treating "coal → graphite" as a universal rule; extending it to diamond; calling igneous "primary" in a chronological rather than a material sense; forgetting that crystalline rock stores water in fractures rather than pores.
- **Answer use:** open a classification answer with the **criterion set**, not with the family names. "Rocks are distinguished by texture, structure, colour, permeability, mode of occurrence and resistance to denudation; on the criterion of genesis they fall into three families" is a first sentence that has already earned structure marks.

Revision notes:

- **Six criteria** (Leong): **texture · structure · colour · permeability · mode of occurrence · resistance to denudation**. Use as the scoring skeleton for any "characteristics and types" directive.
- **Rock = an aggregate of minerals.** Rock-forming minerals: **feldspars, micas, amphiboles, pyroxenes, olivine, quartz, calcite, dolomite, clays, gypsum**.
- **Igneous** = cooled magma. **Plutonic/intrusive** → slow cooling at depth → **large crystals** (granite). **Volcanic/extrusive** → fast cooling at surface → **tiny crystals** (basalt, lava sheets and plateaux).
- Majid classifies igneous rock by **texture + composition** (basalt, gabbro, andesite, diorite, rhyolite, granite) **and** by mode of occurrence.
- "**Primary**" means igneous is the **chief original source of inorganic mineral matter**, not that it comes first in time.
- **Sedimentary** = accumulated sediment, **stratified**, layers from inches to many feet; transport by stream, glacier, wind or organisms.
- **Metamorphic** = pre-existing rock altered by **temperature, pressure and chemically active fluids**; **both texture and mineral composition** change.
- Standard pairs: **shale/mudstone → slate or schist · limestone → marble · sandstone → quartzite · granite → gneiss**.
- ❌ **Foliation ≠ stratification.** Foliation is metamorphic mineral alignment; stratification is sedimentary layering. A schist splits along foliation.
- ⚠️ "Coal → graphite" is an example, not a universal rule; ❗ do not extend it to diamond.
- **Secondary permeability** — joints, fractures, weathered mantle — governs water in crystalline rock, not primary pore space.
- The **rock cycle is closed and has no fixed start**; metamorphic rock can itself be metamorphosed, and any rock can be remelted.
- 🇮🇳 Exemplars: Deccan basalt (extrusive) · Closepet granite (intrusive) · Vindhyan sandstone (mechanical sedimentary) · Gondwana coal (continental sedimentary) · Bengal Gneiss/Khondolite and Charnockite (metamorphic).

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it more accurate to call igneous rock the "primary" rock on grounds of material rather than on grounds of time?

**Model answer:** Because the rock cycle is closed. Igneous rock is produced by the cooling of molten material, and molten material is itself produced by the melting of pre-existing rock at depth or in a subduction setting — so igneous rock is continuously being *re*made from sedimentary and metamorphic parents and cannot be the first rock in any temporal sequence. What is true is the material claim: igneous rock is the chief original source of the inorganic mineral matter that weathering, transport and cementation later assemble into sedimentary rock, and that sedimentary rock in turn supplies many metamorphic parents. "Primary" therefore identifies a position in a chain of *derivation of substance*, which holds at every moment of geological time, rather than a position in a chronology, which holds at none.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the familiar textbook arrow igneous → sedimentary → metamorphic as a one-way timeline. It is one traverse of a loop; metamorphic rock can be metamorphosed again, and any family can be remelted and returned to the start.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain how the physical properties of a rock, rather than its name, determine its influence on landscape and water availability. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** A rock name records an assemblage of minerals; landscape responds to the properties that assemblage produces. Four of the six standard criteria do the work. Texture encodes cooling rate and therefore crystal size, which governs the surface available for chemical attack. Structure determines whether water enters along bedding, along foliation or only along joints. Permeability decides whether rainfall runs off and erodes or percolates and is stored — which is why a permeable limestone can stand as a prominent steep-sided hill while a shale of comparable hardness is worn into low rounded ground. Resistance to denudation, the net outcome of the others, decides which units stand as uplands. Hence the causal variable is permeability and mineral stability rather than hardness alone. The limit is that relief, climate, structure and time act with the rock, so lithology sets a tendency rather than a determined outcome.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for naming and using at least three of the six criteria as explanatory variables; 2 for permeability distinguished from hardness with an example; 2 for the weathering argument run through mineral stability; 2 for the stated limit that lithology is a tendency and not a determinant; 1 for precision of terminology. Deduct 2 for an answer that only lists the three families.

```text
  is EXTRUSIVE and cooled fast -> fine crystals. Everything below is
  INTRUSIVE and cooled slowly -> coarse crystals. One diagram, two types,
```

Draw that section once in an answer and you have supplied the *types* half of the 2022 demand in a form no prose paragraph matches. ⚠️ Note that the diagram also encodes the classification logic: form and texture are both consequences of **where** the magma stopped.

### 1. Why this lesson is the deepest in the world half

This lesson owns a Mains demand outright: **2022, General Studies Paper I, Question 4 — "characteristics and types of primary rocks", Describe, 10 marks, 150 words.** ❗ No solved answer to that question appears anywhere in this file. What appears is the material it requires and, at the end, the structure a 10-mark *Describe* will be marked against.

⚠️ The word **primary** is the hinge. From Lesson 2: igneous rock is primary in the material sense — the chief original source of the inorganic mineral matter of the crust. A script that spends its opening lines proving it is primary in a *chronological* sense has misread the term and will contradict the rock cycle later in its own answer.

### 2. The first division: acid against basic

✅ Leong's discriminator is compositional and it has a **visible** consequence. **Acid** igneous rocks — granite is the type — are **less dense and lighter in colour**. **Basic** igneous rocks contain a greater proportion of **basic oxides: iron, aluminium and magnesium** — and are therefore **denser and darker**.

⚠️ Neither canonical owner carries this discriminator, and it is precisely what a close-option objective item tests, because it converts an invisible chemistry into a visible field property. Hold the pairing as a single sentence: **pale and light = acid = silica-rich = granite; dark and dense = basic = iron-magnesium-rich = basalt and gabbro.**

| | Acid | Basic |
|---|---|---|
| Silica | Higher | Lower |
| Characteristic oxides | Silica-dominated | Iron, aluminium, magnesium |
| Density | Lower | Higher |
| Colour | Lighter | Darker |
| Type examples | Granite, rhyolite | Basalt, gabbro |
| 🇮🇳 Indian case | Closepet granite, Bundelkhand granite | **Deccan Trap basalt** |

⚠️ Now connect it backwards. In Lesson 1, **sial** was granitic with a density of about 2.7 and **sima** basaltic at about 3.0. That is the *same* contrast, read at the scale of the crust instead of the hand specimen. The acid/basic division is therefore not a laboratory nicety; it is the reason continental blocks stand above ocean floors.

### 3. The second division: plutonic against volcanic, and the texture rule

✅ **Plutonic or intrusive** rock cools slowly at depth and grows **large, easily recognised crystals**; it reaches the surface only when denudation strips the cover above it. ✅ **Volcanic or extrusive** rock is erupted and chilled quickly, so crystals are **tiny**.

⚠️ State the rule as a causal proposition, not as a table entry: *crystal size is inversely related to cooling rate, and cooling rate is set by depth of emplacement.* From that one proposition you can derive the texture of any igneous rock whose setting you know, which is more useful than memorising a list.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ "Basalt cools slowly to form large crystals." Basalt is extrusive; it cools fast and is fine-grained. Granite, plutonic, carries the large crystals. The error survives because both rocks are familiar; the cure is to ask *where did it stop?* before asking *what is it called?*

### 4. The forms magma takes — the part that separates a 7 from a 10

✅ Majid enumerates the intrusive forms with explicit size criteria, which makes them examinable in a way that a loose description does not.

- ✅ **Batholith** — the largest intrusive body, **100 sq km or more in extent, with no observable bottom**.
- ✅ **Stock** — the same kind of discordant mass but **under 100 sq km**.
- ✅ **Laccolith** — a dome-shaped intrusion that **arches the overlying strata** into a blister.
- ✅ **Sill** — a sheet injected **along** the bedding, therefore concordant and near-horizontal.
- ✅ **Dike** — a sheet that **cuts across** the bedding, therefore discordant and near-vertical.
- ✅ **Phacolith** — a lens-shaped body occupying the **crest or trough of a fold**; the source associates it with **saddle reefs and gold-quartz veins**, which is why it carries economic weight out of proportion to its size.

✅ On the extrusive side the source separates **explosive** products — **bombs, lapilli, dust and ash** — from **quiet or fissure** eruptions of mobile lava.

⚠️ The fissure type is the one that matters most for India, and Lesson 12 develops it: a flood-basalt province is produced not by a cone but by **mobile basalt issuing from fissures and cracks and spreading as sheets**. 🔁 Eruption style as a *volcanic* subject — cone types, explosivity, volcanic landforms — belongs to Topic 03; what this lesson owns is the fissure flow as an **emplacement mechanism for a rock body**.

### 5. The characteristics, handled with a stated limit

✅ The source sets out an enumerated list of **ten characteristics of igneous rocks**, and a parallel enumeration for the sedimentary and metamorphic families at Lesson 4.

⚠️ **Honest limitation, stated rather than papered over.** The enumerated list was recovered from a source page that this edition could read at summary rather than quotation grade, so the ten items are **not reproduced here as a numbered list**. What is reproduced is the set of characteristics that both canonical owners and the readable source text independently support, which is sufficient for any answer:

| Characteristic | Statement | Where it earns a mark |
|---|---|---|
| Crystalline | Formed by solidification of a melt; the crystal is the diagnostic unit | Separates igneous from clastic sedimentary rock at once |
| Unstratified | No bedding planes, because no deposition occurred | The single quickest field discriminator |
| Non-fossiliferous | Nothing organic survives a melt | Why igneous rock cannot be dated by fossils — L4 |
| Massive and jointed | Cools as a coherent body; contraction produces joints | Joints, not pores, carry the water — L2 and L14 |
| Low primary permeability | Interlocking crystals leave little pore space | The basis of the Deccan aquifer problem — L14 |
| Classified by texture **and** composition | Two independent axes, not one | Produces the four-box grid below |
| Source of primary minerals | The original supplier of the crust's inorganic matter | The sense in which it is "primary" |

⚠️ Combining the two axes gives a grid that answers a "types" directive completely:

| | **Acid** (pale, less dense) | **Basic** (dark, denser) |
|---|---|---|
| **Plutonic** — coarse crystals | Granite | Gabbro |
| **Volcanic** — fine crystals | Rhyolite | Basalt |

### 6. Rock type as a landscape control, demonstrated rather than asserted

✅ Leong makes the petrology-to-relief link with worked cases: **resistant granites form high ground**; **quartzite forms the highest peak** in his example — Gunong Tahan, 7,186 ft; **shales and schists are worn into low rounded hills**; and **permeable limestone forms prominent steep-sided hills**.

⚠️ Read the fourth case carefully, because it is the one that carries the argument. Limestone is **not** harder than schist. It stands up because it is **permeable**: rainfall sinks into it instead of running across it, so surface erosion is slow. ❗ The causal variable is **permeability, not hardness alone** — a distinction the examiner has tested, and one that reappears at Lesson 14 when the Peninsular uplands are explained.

⚠️ Leong's measurements here are **imperial and mid-century**; quote the example, attribute the figure, and do not convert it silently.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — one province, two igneous types, both named

⚠️ India supplies an unusually clean pair.

- ✅ The **Deccan Trap** is the extrusive case at provincial scale: **fissure-fed, extremely mobile basalt** spreading as sheets to build a flat-topped, steep-sided, stepped terrain. It is **basic** — dark, dense, iron- and magnesium-rich — and its weathering **produces** the black cotton soil. 🔁 The soil itself is Topic 04's and this edition stops at the word *produces*.
- ✅ **Closepet granite**, named from the Closepet belt in the Dharwar terrain of Karnataka, is the intrusive case: **acid**, pale, coarse-grained, emplaced at depth and exposed by prolonged denudation of the shield.

⚠️ The pair is worth more than either member, because it demonstrates the texture rule inside one country: the same magmatic process produced coarse granite where it stopped at depth in the Archaean basement, and fine basalt where it reached the surface at the close of the Cretaceous.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ "The Deccan Trap is a sedimentary formation." It is **igneous — flood basalt, extrusive volcanic rock**. The error is almost always caused by the stepped, layered appearance of trap country, which looks like bedding and is not: the "steps" are **successive lava flows**, which is why the word *trap* was borrowed from the Swedish for stair.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Plutonic | Volcanic |
|---|---|---|
| Where it solidified | At depth | At or above the surface |
| Cooling rate | Slow | Fast |
| Texture | Coarse, crystals visible | Fine, crystals often invisible |
| Typical forms | Batholith, stock, laccolith, sill, dike, phacolith | Flow, sheet, plateau, cone |
| Exposure | Only after denudation removes the cover | Immediate |
| Indian case | Closepet granite | Deccan Trap basalt |

**Strongest objection.** The acid/basic division is chemistry, and a geography paper examines landscape. Is it not surplus?

**Reply.** It is not, because the division has **three visible consequences** that are pure geography. It fixes **colour**, which is how the rock is identified in the field. It fixes **density**, which is why granitic continental crust stands above basaltic ocean floor — the sial/sima contrast of Lesson 1 and the isostatic argument of Lesson 9. And it correlates with **eruptive behaviour**, basic magmas being the mobile ones that spread as sheets, which is the entire reason the Deccan is a plateau rather than a chain of cones. A chemical division with three geographic consequences is geography.

⚠️ **Residual.** Texture is a reliable guide to cooling rate but not an infallible one: a single body can be coarse at its centre and fine at its chilled margin, and some rocks cooled in two stages. An answer should say "generally" when it moves from texture to depth, and should not promise a one-to-one correspondence.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership); Prelims physical geography.
- **Verified linkage:** **2022 GS-I Q4 — characteristics and types of primary rocks, Describe, 10 marks, 150 words.** This lesson is the owner. The demand is a Mains demand, so no key issue arises; ❗ no solved model of it appears here.
- **Approach the directive licenses:** *Describe* wants ordered content, not evaluation. Define "primary" in the material sense in one clause; give the characteristics through the criterion frame of Lesson 2; give the types on **two axes** — acid/basic and plutonic/volcanic — rather than one; name the intrusive and extrusive **forms** with their size criteria, because that is where most scripts stop short; close with one Indian case on each side. ❗ Do not spend lines on the rock cycle; the question did not ask for it.
- **Traps:** basalt given coarse crystals; the Deccan called sedimentary; "primary" argued chronologically; batholith and stock separated by shape rather than by the **100 sq km** criterion; sill and dike distinguished by orientation alone rather than by **concordance with bedding**.
- **Answer use:** the section diagram at the head of this lesson is reproducible in under a minute and carries six named forms; in a *Describe* answer at 10 marks it is the highest-yield single object you can put on the page.

Revision notes:

- **Primary = material sense**: igneous rock is the chief original source of the crust's inorganic mineral matter. ❗ Not a chronological claim.
- **Acid** — silica-rich, **less dense, lighter in colour** (granite, rhyolite). **Basic** — richer in **iron, aluminium, magnesium** oxides, **denser and darker** (basalt, gabbro).
- The same contrast at crustal scale is **sial (≈2.7) over sima (≈3.0)**.
- **Texture rule:** crystal size is inversely related to cooling rate; cooling rate is set by depth of emplacement. Plutonic = coarse; volcanic = fine.
- **Two-axis grid:** granite (acid plutonic) · gabbro (basic plutonic) · rhyolite (acid volcanic) · **basalt (basic volcanic)**.
- **Batholith ≥ 100 sq km, no observable bottom**; **stock < 100 sq km**; **laccolith** domes the strata; **sill** concordant along bedding; **dike** discordant across bedding; **phacolith** in a fold crest or trough — **saddle reefs, gold-quartz veins**.
- Extrusive products: explosive — **bombs, lapilli, dust, ash**; quiet/**fissure** — mobile lava in sheets.
- Igneous characteristics: crystalline · **unstratified** · **non-fossiliferous** · massive and jointed · low primary permeability · classified on **texture and composition** · the source of primary minerals.
- **Landscape control:** resistant granite → high ground; quartzite → the highest peak in Leong's example; shale and schist → low rounded hills; **permeable limestone → prominent steep-sided hills**. ❗ The variable is **permeability**, not hardness alone.
- 🇮🇳 **Deccan Trap** = basic, extrusive, fissure-fed; **Closepet granite** = acid, plutonic, exposed by denudation.
- ❌ Deccan Trap as sedimentary — the "steps" are **successive lava flows**, hence *trap*, from the Swedish for stair.
- 🔁 Eruption style, cone types and volcanic hazard → Topic 03. Black soil as a soil → Topic 04.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two Indian rocks — Closepet granite and Deccan basalt — share an igneous origin yet differ in colour, density, crystal size and the landform they build. Explain each difference from a single underlying variable in each case, and state what the two variables are.

**Model answer:** Two variables suffice. **Composition** explains colour and density: the granite is acid, silica-rich and poorer in basic oxides, so it is pale and less dense, while the basalt is basic, richer in iron and magnesium, so it is dark and denser. **Depth of emplacement** explains crystal size and landform: the granite solidified slowly at depth, so its crystals grew large and the body is exposed only because prolonged denudation of the Archaean shield has stripped the cover above it, whereas the basalt was erupted at the surface through fissures and chilled quickly, so its crystals are minute and it spread laterally as sheets to build a stepped plateau rather than a dome or a cone. Composition is inherited from the melt; depth is a matter of where the melt stopped.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the Deccan's stepped profile as evidence of bedding and therefore of sedimentary origin. The steps are successive flows of a mobile basic lava, each with a flat top and a steep front — an extrusive igneous structure that imitates stratification without being it.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Describe the forms assumed by intrusive igneous bodies, and explain what the form of an intrusion reveals about the conditions of its emplacement. *(Describe and explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Intrusive bodies are classified by size and by their relation to the host structure. A batholith is the largest, at least 100 sq km in extent and with no observable bottom; a stock is a similar discordant mass under 100 sq km. A laccolith is dome-shaped and arches the overlying strata into a blister. A sill is injected along the bedding and is therefore concordant and near-horizontal; a dike cuts across the bedding and is discordant and near-vertical. A phacolith occupies the crest or trough of a fold, and is economically notable for saddle reefs and gold-quartz veins. Form is diagnostic. Concordance shows that the magma exploited an existing plane of weakness; discordance shows that it forced a new path. Doming indicates that pressure exceeded the load above. Coarse texture throughout indicates slow cooling, so an exposed batholith also records a long subsequent history of denudation.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 4 for six forms named with their defining criteria, including the **100 sq km** batholith-stock threshold; 2 for concordance against discordance used as the organising principle rather than orientation alone; 2 for form read as evidence of emplacement conditions; 1 for the texture-to-cooling-rate inference; 1 for the denudation inference attached to exposed plutons. Deduct 2 if a sill and a dike are distinguished only as horizontal and vertical.

## Lesson 4 — Secondary and tertiary rocks: sedimentary classes, metamorphic pairs, and the chronology toolkit

```text
                                                                   POTASH | NITRATES

     water -> aqueous/argillaceous: MARINE | LACUSTRINE | RIVERINE

              and the ABSENCE of stratification is what proves wind origin;
              absent in the equatorial tropics
     ice   -> glacial: MORAINES
```

```text
                 THE STRATIGRAPHIC COLUMN AS A CLOCK
   ______________________________________________________________
  |  4  horizontal, undisturbed beds        <- youngest           |
  |_____________________________________________________________ |
  |/\/\/\/\/\/\/  U N C O N F O R M I T Y  /\/\/\/\/\/\/\/\/\/\/\ |  <- the
  |  3  tilted, folded, truncated beds                           |     ERASED
  |  2  older beds, same deformation                             |     INTERVAL
  |  1  basement, crystalline                 <- oldest          |
  |______________________________________________________________|

  WHAT THE SURFACE RECORDS, IN ORDER:
  deposition of 1-3  ->  deformation (tilting/folding)  ->  UPLIFT  ->
  DENUDATION of the folded surface  ->  SUBMERGENCE  ->  deposition of 4

  An unconformity is therefore not a gap in the rock. It is a gap in TIME,
  and it is the only structure that records an event by its ABSENCE.
```

Two visuals, because this lesson has two jobs: sorting rocks by origin, and converting a pile of rock into a sequence of events. ⚠️ The second job is the one the India half depends on completely, and it is the one most summaries omit.

### I. Sedimentary rock sorted by origin

✅ Leong's classification is by **origin of the material**, and each class carries worked examples that an answer can name.

- ✅ **Mechanically formed.** Fragments of pre-existing rock, transported and cemented. **Sandstone** is the type; **grit** is the coarser variety; **conglomerate** is made of **rounded** pebbles and **breccia** of **angular** ones; finer products are **clay, shale and mudstone**.
- ✅ **Organically formed**, and it splits in two. **Calcareous** rocks — **limestone and chalk** — are built from **corals and shellfish**. **Carbonaceous** rocks — **peat, lignite and coal** — are built from **vegetative matter**.
- ✅ **Chemically formed.** Precipitates from solution, typically by evaporation: **rock salt**, **gypsum** of the Dead Sea type, **potash** and **nitrates**.

⚠️ Two deductions are available free of charge and both are examinable. First, the **conglomerate/breccia pair is a transport indicator**: rounding takes distance, so rounded pebbles imply a long journey and angular fragments imply a short one. Second, the **calcareous/carbonaceous split is the geological foundation of India's two great sedimentary resources** — carbonaceous accumulation is why the Gondwana basins hold coal (Lesson 12), and calcareous deposition in Purana basins is why limestone and building stone come from where they do (Lesson 11).

✅ Majid cuts the same material a second way, **by transporting agent**. Water-laid, **argillaceous or aqueous**, types are **marine, lacustrine or riverine**. Wind-laid material gives **loess**. Ice-laid material gives **moraines**. ✅ Sub-aerially exposed riverine sedimentary rock develops **polygonal desiccation cracks** on drying.

✅ **Loess repays close reading.** It is **coherent but not cemented**; it is **permeable**; its **lack of stratification is what proves an aeolian origin**; and it is **absent in the equatorial tropics**. ⚠️ The middle point inverts the usual rule. Sedimentary rock is normally identified *by* its stratification; loess is identified by the **absence** of it, because wind deposits a uniform fine silt without the sorting a current imposes. ✅ The source's worked case is the loess of northern China.

### II. Metamorphic rock read backwards

✅ Metamorphic rock is produced from pre-existing rock by changes in **temperature, pressure and the chemical action of fluids**, which alter **texture and mineral composition together**. ✅ The common products named are **phyllite, quartzite, schist and slate**, with a figure devoted to the origins of common metamorphic rocks. ✅ **Gneiss** shows granular quartz-feldspar banding; **slate** shows slaty cleavage; both are field-identifiable on that basis.

| Parent | Product | What the change does | Reversibility of the inference |
|---|---|---|---|
| Limestone or dolostone | **Marble** | Recrystallisation of carbonate | Parent obvious |
| Sandstone | **Quartzite** | Quartz grains fused; hardness rises sharply | Parent obvious |
| Shale or mudstone | **Slate** (low grade) → **phyllite** → **schist** (higher grade) | Progressive alignment of platy minerals | Parent usually recoverable |
| Granite or other felsic rock | **Gneiss** | Segregation into light and dark bands | Parent often **not** recoverable |
| Carbon-rich material | **Graphite** | Loss of volatiles, ordering of carbon | ⚠️ An example, not a universal rule |

⚠️ The right-hand column is the part worth marks. ✅ The Core owner states plainly that for quartzite and marble the source rock is clear, while **for schist and gneiss the parent is often hard to determine**. An answer that claims every metamorphic rock announces its parent has overclaimed; one that distinguishes the easy cases from the hard ones has shown control.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ "Marble is an igneous rock." It is metamorphic, from limestone under heat and pressure, and the error is caused by its crystalline sparkle, which looks igneous. ⚠️ The cure is structural: marble is **massive and recrystallised but carries no igneous texture and no bedding**, and its chemistry is carbonate, not silicate.

> 🔑 **Second trap.** ❌ "Metamorphic rock permeability follows from the rock's name." It does not. ✅ **Fracturing, jointing and weathering control secondary permeability more than the unfractured matrix does** — the point installed at Lesson 2 and spent at Lesson 14.

### III. The chronology toolkit — the real reason this lesson exists

⚠️ Everything in the India half is a claim about **order in time**, and order in time is read from rock by four devices. Three of them come straight out of the two families just taught.

1. **Stratification and superposition.** ✅ Sedimentary rock is **always layered**. ⚠️ In an undisturbed sequence the lower bed was laid first, so the stack is a sequence. This is the device the entire Indian ladder rests on.
2. **Fossils.** ✅ Sedimentary rock may contain organic remains; ✅ igneous rock cannot, because nothing organic survives a melt. ⚠️ Where fossils are present, beds can be correlated between distant basins; where they are **absent**, as in the unfossiliferous Purana sequences of Lesson 11, correlation becomes far harder and dates become far softer. ❗ That is why Indian Proterozoic dates are quoted here with wide brackets and always with their source.
3. **The unconformity.** ⚠️ A surface separating two sets of strata where the lower set was deposited, deformed, uplifted and **eroded** before the upper set was laid down. It records an interval during which rock was being destroyed rather than made — so it marks **time that has no rock to represent it**. ✅ This is the structure that at Lesson 11 separates the Dharwars from the Puranas, and the Lower Vindhyan from the Upper.
4. **Cross-cutting relations.** ⚠️ A dike is younger than everything it cuts; an intrusion is younger than its host. ⚠️ This is the one device that works in igneous and metamorphic terrain where neither bedding nor fossils are available — which is to say, across most of the Indian shield.

⚠️ **The honest limitation, which belongs in any chronology paragraph.** These devices establish **relative** order, not absolute age. Absolute ages come from radiometric dating, and the Indian figures quoted later in this file are the published figures of named authors rather than independent measurements made here. ❗ Quote them with their source attached, or not at all.

### IV. 🇮🇳 India hook — two Indian structures that are nothing but this lesson applied

⚠️ Two Indian cases are the chronology toolkit made visible, and both are developed later.

- ✅ **The Vindhyan unconformity.** The Vindhyan sequence is split into a **Lower** division that is marine, calcareous and **tectonically folded**, and an **Upper** division that is fluviatile and lies in **gently disposed, undisturbed horizontal strata**. The two are separated by an unconformity whose prominence is itself regionally variable — pronounced in the north, almost absent in southern Mewar, Chittaurgarh and the Son valley. ⚠️ Read structurally: between the two divisions the lower beds were folded, uplifted and planed off, and only then were the upper beds laid flat across the truncated edges. Lesson 11 develops the consequences.
- ✅ **Khondolite and Charnockite** are India's named metamorphic series. **Khondolite** is the Eastern Ghats name for a very thinly foliated gneiss, taken from the **Khond tribes** of Koraput and Bolangir; it is the same rock first identified in **Midnapur district** and known there as **Bengal Gneiss**. **Charnockite** is a bluish-grey to dark plutonic gneiss of the Nilgiri type, named in honour of **Job Charnock, whose tombstone in Kolkata was made of this rock**. ⚠️ Both are Archaean, and Lesson 10 places them.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Sedimentary | Metamorphic |
|---|---|---|
| Produced by | Deposition and cementation | Heat, pressure and chemically active fluids |
| Diagnostic structure | **Stratification** (bedding) | **Foliation** (mineral alignment) |
| Fossils | Possible, and the basis of correlation | Generally destroyed |
| Parent | Not applicable; the material is derived | Sometimes obvious, sometimes unrecoverable |
| Primary permeability | Often high, especially sandstone | Generally low; joints and fractures take over |
| Time information carried | Order of deposition; unconformities | Order of events, through cross-cutting and grade |

**Strongest objection.** Sedimentary and metamorphic rock are "secondary" and "tertiary". Does the Mains syllabus not confine itself to the primary family, which is what 2022 actually asked about?

**Reply.** No, for two reasons. The 2022 demand was a *primary*-rock demand, but the answer's opening classification sentence must situate the primary family against the other two, and a candidate who cannot describe what sedimentary and metamorphic rock *are* cannot define the contrast. More decisively, **the whole India half of this topic is a sedimentary and metamorphic story** — an Archaean metamorphic basement, Purana sedimentary basins, Gondwana continental sediments, Tertiary molasse — interrupted by one great igneous episode. Teach only the igneous family and the file loses its second half.

⚠️ **Residual.** The three-way origin classification is clean in the textbook and messy in the field: many real rocks are mixed, a limestone can be partly chemical and partly organic, and an "unconformity" can be a complex surface rather than a single plane. Use the classification as an organising device and avoid asserting that any particular Indian bed is purely one class unless a source says so.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** supports **2022 GS-I Q4** by supplying the contrast class; supplies the unconformity reasoning on which the 2026 Peninsular Block demand's chronology depends.
- **Probable framing:** as the second paragraph of a classification answer, and as the entire toolkit paragraph of any question that asks how a geological sequence is established.
- **Traps:** marble called igneous; foliation equated with stratification; loess expected to be stratified; metamorphic permeability read off the rock name; absolute ages asserted where only relative order is available.
- **Answer use:** when a question asks how the age sequence of Indian rock systems is known, do not list dates. Write the **device**: superposition in undisturbed sequences, fossil correlation where fossils exist, unconformities as recorded gaps, and cross-cutting relations in crystalline terrain where the first two fail.

Revision notes:

- **Three sedimentary origins:** **mechanically formed** (sandstone; **grit** coarser; **conglomerate** rounded; **breccia** angular; clay, shale, mudstone) · **organically formed** — **calcareous** (limestone, chalk, from corals and shellfish) and **carbonaceous** (peat, lignite, coal, from vegetative matter) · **chemically formed** (rock salt, gypsum, potash, nitrates).
- **By agent:** water → **marine, lacustrine, riverine**; wind → **loess**; ice → **moraines**. Desiccation cracks mark sub-aerial riverine exposure.
- **Loess:** coherent but **not cemented**, **permeable**, **absence of stratification proves aeolian origin**, **absent in the equatorial tropics**; type case northern China.
- **Conglomerate vs breccia = distance of transport.** Rounding takes travel.
- **Metamorphic pairs:** limestone → **marble** · sandstone → **quartzite** · shale → **slate → phyllite → schist** by rising grade · granite → **gneiss**. ⚠️ Coal → graphite is an example, not a universal rule; ❗ never extended to diamond.
- Parent **recoverable** for marble and quartzite; **often not** for schist and gneiss.
- **Gneiss** = granular quartz-feldspar banding; **slate** = slaty cleavage.
- **Four chronology devices:** stratification and **superposition** · **fossils** (absent in igneous rock; absent in unfossiliferous Purana beds, so dates soften) · **unconformity** = deposition → deformation → uplift → **erosion** → renewed deposition, recording **time without rock** · **cross-cutting relations**, the only device that works in crystalline terrain.
- These give **relative** order; absolute ages are **quoted from named authors**, never asserted.
- 🇮🇳 **Vindhyan unconformity:** Lower = marine, calcareous, **folded**; Upper = fluviatile, **horizontal, undisturbed**; the break is prominent in the north, nearly absent in southern Mewar, Chittaurgarh and the Son valley.
- 🇮🇳 **Khondolite** (Eastern Ghats name, from the **Khond** tribes of Koraput and Bolangir; the **Bengal Gneiss** first identified in **Midnapur**) and **Charnockite** (named for **Job Charnock**, whose Kolkata tombstone is of this rock).

### Concept check

**Question:** An unconformity is sometimes described as "a gap in the record". Explain why it is better described as a recorded event, and say what sequence of events a single unconformity surface actually preserves.

**Model answer:** Calling it a gap suggests that nothing is known about the interval, whereas the surface itself is evidence of a definite sequence. For beds below an unconformity to be truncated, four things must have happened in order: the lower beds were deposited; they were deformed, tilted or folded; they were uplifted into the zone of denudation; and they were eroded so that the folded structures were planed off. Only then could the upper beds be laid down across the bevelled edges, which usually requires renewed submergence or subsidence. So the surface records deposition, deformation, uplift, erosion and renewed deposition — five events — even though it preserves no rock from the interval. What is missing is the *material*, not the information. That is why an unconformity is the most informative single surface in a stratigraphic column, and why the break between the Lower and Upper Vindhyan carries more meaning than either division's thickness.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the magnitude of the time break as constant along the surface. The Vindhyan case shows the opposite: the same unconformity is prominent in the north and almost absent in parts of southern Mewar, Chittaurgarh and the Son valley, so the length of the erased interval varies regionally.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Sedimentary rocks are classified twice over — once by the origin of their material and once by the agent that carried it." Discuss, with examples, and state what each classification explains that the other does not. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Both classifications are in use because they answer different questions. By origin of material, sedimentary rocks are mechanically formed, from fragments of older rock — sandstone, grit, conglomerate of rounded pebbles, breccia of angular ones, clay and shale; organically formed, either calcareous from corals and shellfish, as limestone and chalk, or carbonaceous from vegetation, as peat, lignite and coal; or chemically formed by precipitation, as rock salt, gypsum, potash and nitrates. By agent, the same material is aqueous — marine, lacustrine or riverine — aeolian, or glacial. Origin explains the rock's composition and therefore its economic value: carbonaceous accumulation is why coal exists where it does. Agent explains its texture, sorting and structure: loess is coherent but uncemented, permeable, and identified by the *absence* of stratification, while glacial moraine is unsorted. Neither alone is sufficient, since a limestone's value follows from origin and its bedding from agent.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the three-way origin classification with at least five named examples; 2 for the agent classification with the aqueous sub-types; 2 for the explanatory division of labour stated explicitly; 2 for loess used correctly, including the absence-of-stratification criterion; 1 for the conglomerate-breccia transport inference. Deduct 2 for presenting the two classifications as rivals rather than as complements.

## Lesson 5 — Crustal movement: folding, faulting, and the orogenic chronology of the earth

Leong, local-scan pp.19-22 (nine orogenic movements with dates and named ranges; Fig. 17 structural divisions of the earth; the fold-geometry progression at Figs. 18-19; block mountains, rift valleys and the four-class typology; plateau types).

```text
  THE OROGENIC TIME RAIL -- read right to left, oldest to youngest
  =================================================================
  3,500 Ma |||||||||||||| PRE-CAMBRIAN OROGENIES ||||||||| 600 Ma
           (Leong: nine orogenic movements since the dawn of
            geological time; the Pre-Cambrian ones fall between
            600 and 3,500 Ma)                   ANCIENT SHIELDS
                                                        |
                                 ~320 Ma   CALEDONIAN ---+
                                 Scandinavia | Scotland |

                                 Urals | Pennines | Welsh Highlands |
```
