Book context: Advanced owner §1-§2 and §4-§5 (Gondwana basins and bituminous coal; the Deccan Trap as a flood-basalt province; the two trap traps); D.R. Khullar, Chapter 2, printed pp.45-48 (Gondwana as a rift-basin system with its four Peninsular belts; the Mesozoic marine systems; the Deccan Trap with the denudation correction and the threefold division); Majid Husain, *Geography of India*, Chapter 1, printed pp.1.9-1.11 (Gondwana sub-series and the coal seams; the nine-step Aryan chronology).
CA search: "Geological Survey of India field season 2025-26 Deccan Traps geochemical mapping natural hydrogen Andaman" — run for this lesson.
CA found: **Geological Survey of India concludes Field Season 2025-26** — Press Information Bureau / Ministry of Mines, **2 April 2026**, in the GSI's **175th year**. Verified across independent citing reports; developed below as this file's single current linkage.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
      A LOADED TROUGH -- why Gondwana sequences are so thick
  ===============================================================
  STAGE 1   an ancient PLATEAU SURFACE, already stabilised
            ________________________________________________
           |                                                |
           |              ARCHAEAN / DHARWAR                |
           |________________________________________________|

  STAGE 2   GEOSYNCLINAL TROUGHS open on that surface;
            continental, FLUVIATILE and LACUSTRINE sediment
            begins to accumulate
            _______      ____________      ______
           |       \____/            \____/      |
           |   trough        trough       trough |
           |____________________________________ |

  STAGE 3   AS SEDIMENT ACCUMULATES THE LOADED TROUGH SUBSIDES.
            The floor goes down as fast as the fill comes in, so
            very thick FRESHWATER and SUBAERIAL sequences can
            accumulate without ever being drowned.
                 |    |    |    |    |    |
                 v    v    v    v    v    v
            ~~~~~~~~ about 6,000 m of flat strata ~~~~~~~~
            from the start of the PERMIAN, ~250 Ma
  ===============================================================
  >> The mechanism matters: NOT a deep basin that was slowly
     filled, but a SHALLOW ONE THAT KEPT SINKING. That is how a
     continental, non-marine sequence reaches 6,000 m.
```

```text
     DECCAN TRAP THICKNESS PROFILE  [asset M7]  -- west to east
  ===============================================================
   MUMBAI COAST        KACHCHH      SOUTHERN LIMIT    AMARKANTAK
   ~3,000 m            800 m        600-800 m         only 150 m
   ##########                                           .
   ##########           ###                              eastern
   ##########           ###          ####                limit
   ##########           ###          ####
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   Individual flows: from a fraction of a metre to 36 m.

   THREEFOLD DIVISION
   UPPER TRAP   Maharashtra and Saurashtra | numerous INTER-TRAPPEAN
                beds and ASH layers | average ~450 m
   MIDDLE TRAP  Central India and Malwa | ~1,200 m | ash-beds above |
                practically DEVOID of inter-trappeans
   LOWER TRAP   Central India and Tamil Nadu | only ~150 m |
                inter-trappean beds present but ash RARE
   ===============================================================
   !! THE CORRECTION THAT MOST ANSWERS MISS:
      ORIGINAL EXTENT ~ TEN LAKH sq km.
      DENUDATION HAS SINCE REDUCED THE PROVINCE TO ABOUT HALF.
      PRESENT EXTENT ~ FIVE LAKH sq km.
      Quoting five lakh as the eruption's extent is WRONG.
```

⚠️ A block diagram and a profile, because the two halves of this lesson are mechanically different: one is a story about **subsidence**, the other about **accumulation then removal**.

### Movement I — Where the Aryan system sits, and the nine-step spine

✅ The **Aryan** system is the fourth and uppermost rung of the scheme, running from the Gondwana through the Deccan Trap and the Tertiary to the Quaternary. ⚠️ Lessons 12 and 13 divide it at the **Mesozoic/Cenozoic boundary**, which is also the Deccan eruption, because that is where India's geological history changes subject from a fragmenting supercontinent to a rising mountain chain.

✅ The whole Aryan interval has an ordered nine-step spine, and it is worth carrying as a single sequence:

1. ✅ In the **Upper Carboniferous** the Himalayan region was occupied by the vast **Tethys geosyncline**, connected **eastward to the Pacific via China** and **westward to the Atlantic via Afghanistan, Iran, Asia Minor and the present Mediterranean**.
2. ✅ **Violent volcanic activity** in the Kashmir Himalayas.
3. ✅ **Fissuring of Gondwanaland** and the **northward drift of the Indian subcontinent** to collide with Eurasia.
4. ✅ **Large-scale lava eruption** forming the **Deccan Trap**.
5. ✅ **Development and expansion of the Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal**.
6. ✅ **Tertiary mountain-building** giving birth to the **Himalayas**.
7. ✅ India **assuming its present shape**.
8. ✅ The onset of the **Pleistocene Ice Age**.
9. ✅ The **evolution and spread of man**.

⚠️ Steps 1 to 5 are this lesson. Steps 6 to 9 are Lesson 13. ❗ Note that the spine gets the order right where one local source does not: **Pangaea assembled in the late Palaeozoic and broke up in the Mesozoic**, so assembly and break-up are separate events on opposite sides of the Permo-Triassic boundary, and this edition keeps them there.

### Movement II — Gondwana as a rift-basin system, not a coal seam

✅ The Gondwana rocks are **continental, fluviatile and lacustrine deposits laid in geosynclinal troughs on an ancient plateau surface**; ✅ as sediment accumulated the **loaded troughs subsided**, which allowed very thick freshwater and subaerial sequences to accumulate — ✅ flat sedimentary strata **some 6,000 m thick**, from the start of the **Permian, about 250 Ma**.

⚠️ That subsidence mechanism is the lesson's structural content and it is what most summaries omit. A thick **continental** sequence is a puzzle: continental environments are shallow, and shallow environments cannot accumulate six kilometres of sediment unless the floor keeps going down. **The load itself drives the subsidence**, which is why these particular basins are so deep and why their coal seams are so thick.

✅ The name comes from the **Kingdom of the Gonds**, the rocks having been first discovered in the Gond region of Madhya Pradesh.

✅ **Four Peninsular belts**, and they should be learnt as a set:

| Belt | Location |
|---|---|
| **Damodar valley** | Jharkhand |
| **Mahanadi valley** | Chhattisgarh and Odisha |
| **Southern Madhya Pradesh** | — |
| **A series of troughs along the Godavari** | From **Nagpur to the delta** |

✅ In the extra-Peninsula, Gondwana rocks occur in **Kashmir, Darjeeling and Sikkim**.

✅ **Sub-series:** the **Lower Gondwana comprises the Talcher, Panchet and Damuda** groups. **Talcher** is named after Talcher in Dhenkanal district, Odisha. ✅ The **Damuda** carries the thicker eastern seams — **Raniganj, Jharia, Karanpura, Bokaro, Singrauli, Korba, Pench, Talcher and Singareni** — and ✅ the **Jhingurda seam, about 131 m, is the thickest coal seam in India**.

⚠️ **One normalisation applied.** A local source places **Singareni** in the "Satpura basin, Madhya Pradesh". ❗ Singareni lies in the **Godavari valley**, which is also where the four-belt list above puts it, and that is where this edition places it.

### Movement III — The statistic, and the two errors it prevents

✅ **Gondwana rocks are economically the most important in India, containing about 98 per cent of her coal reserves.**

⚠️ That single sentence is the most quotable Indian resource statistic in the whole topic, and it kills two standard errors at once.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ "Most Indian coal is in the Deccan Trap." ✅ **Almost all Indian coal is in Gondwana rocks**, not the Deccan Trap. ⚠️ The Deccan is **igneous**; coal is a **carbonaceous sedimentary** rock, and the two cannot be the same formation. ✅ Tertiary coal is important in the **north-east**, which is a separate and much smaller story taken up at Lesson 13.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ Placing the Gondwana system inside the **Dravidian**. ✅ Gondwana is **Aryan**. ⚠️ The stratigraphic reason was given at Lesson 11: the Dravidian is substantially absent from the Peninsula, while the Gondwana is the Peninsula's defining Aryan sequence.

❗ **And a standing data caution.** ⚠️ The 98 per cent figure is a **structural proportion stated in the source**, not a current reserve statistic, and ❗ **book-era production and reserve tables are excluded from this edition entirely**. Say "the overwhelming share, on the order of 98 per cent as stated in the standard account", and attach the attribution.

### Movement IV — The Mesozoic marine systems, briefly and with one exclusion

✅ Three Mesozoic systems complete the pre-Deccan picture:

- ✅ **Triassic (280-225 Ma)** — almost unknown in the Peninsula, exposed from **Hazara to Nepal**. ❗ **No Triassic thickness is quoted in this edition**, because the source passage contains an internal inconsistency on that point.
- ✅ **Jurassic (180 Ma)**, named after the **Jura** mountains — a Jurassic tract of about **190 × 64 km** in **Kachchh**, and the **Jaisalmer** rocks of Rajasthan.
- ✅ **Cretaceous (135-70 Ma)**, from *creta*, chalk — and ✅ **"no other system is so widely distributed in India"**, with the **Bagh Beds** near Gwalior underlying the Trap and the estuarine and lacustrine **Lametas** in the central Peninsula.

⚠️ The Bagh Beds and the Lametas matter for one reason: they lie **beneath** the Deccan flows and therefore **date the eruption from below**. A lava that covers Cretaceous sediment cannot be older than it.

### Movement V — The Deccan Trap, with the correction that most answers miss

✅ From the end of the **Cretaceous** to the beginning of the **Eocene**, **about ten lakh sq km** was flooded by **extremely mobile basalt issuing from fissures and cracks**, ✅ **covering fully the pre-existing topography**. ✅ The flat tops and steep sides of the resulting flows make them look like **gigantic steps**, hence **'trap'**, from the **Swedish for stair or step**.

❗ ✅ **Denudation has since reduced the province to about half, and the present Deccan Trap covers about five lakh sq km.**

⚠️ **This is the single highest-frequency Indian error in the topic.** Quoting the present ~5 lakh sq km as the extent of the eruption understates the event by half and misses the entire point that the Deccan is a **denudation residue** — which is also why Lesson 5 could classify the Deccan Plateau among **residual mountains, mountains of denudation**. The two statements are the same fact.

✅ **Individual flows vary from a fraction of a metre to 36 m.** ✅ Thickness varies systematically: **about 3,000 m along the Mumbai coast**, **600-800 m at the southern limit**, **800 m in Kachchh**, and **only 150 m at Amarkantak** at the eastern limit. ⚠️ Read the profile west to east and the province is a **wedge thinning away from its western source region** — which is a structural statement, not a list of numbers.

✅ **Threefold division:** the **Upper Trap** of Maharashtra and Saurashtra, with numerous **inter-trappean beds and ash layers**, averaging about **450 m**; the **Middle Trap** of Central India and Malwa, about **1,200 m**, with ash-beds above and **practically devoid of inter-trappeans**; and the **Lower Trap** of Central India and Tamil Nadu, only about **150 m**, with inter-trappean beds present but ash rare.

⚠️ **Inter-trappean beds are the diagnostic feature**, because they are **sediment laid between successive lava flows** — which proves that the eruption was **episodic**, with intervals long enough for deposition. A province of continuous outpouring would have none.

✅ **Spate prefers "Deccan Lava" to "Deccan Trap"** — a nomenclature preference worth naming, since "trap" describes a landform and "lava" describes the rock.

✅ Weathering of Deccan basalt over long periods **produces regur**, the black cotton soil. 🔁 The soil itself — its properties, its agriculture, its management — belongs to the weathering-and-soils owner, and this edition stops at the word *produces*.

### Movement VI — 📰 The province as a live object of national survey

📰 **The Geological Survey of India concluded Field Season 2025-26** — Press Information Bureau / Ministry of Mines, **2 April 2026**, in the organisation's **175th year**. The verified elements relevant here:

| Verified element | Value |
|---|---|
| Mineral exploration projects undertaken | **458** |
| Of which targeting critical minerals | **230** |
| Of which targeting rare earth elements | **92** |
| Geological reports handed over for auction | **80** |
| Large-scale geological mapping | **about 22,000 sq km** |
| **Geochemical mapping in the Deccan Traps** | **28,000 sq km** |
| New resource discovery | **Naturally occurring hydrogen in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands** |
| Methods newly adopted | **Drone-based magnetic surveys; AI-driven mineral targeting** |

⚠️ **Why this item belongs to Topic 02 and not to the resource topic.** The decisive element is the **28,000 sq km of geochemical mapping inside the Deccan Traps** — a systematic survey of a **flood-basalt province**, which is a geological-structure object and precisely the object this lesson teaches. The **natural-hydrogen find in the Andamans** is likewise a statement about the geology of the **extra-Peninsular arc**, which Lesson 9 places inside the extra-Peninsular region.

🔁 **And where the restraint falls.** The exploration-project counts and the auction-ready reports are **resource-economy facts**: they are named once here and **routed** to the mineral-and-energy-resources topic and to the science-and-technology folder. ❗ They are not developed, and no inference is drawn here about reserves, auctions, supply chains or policy.

⚠️ **The answer-level point is small and good:** the Deccan is not a museum fact. It is a flood-basalt province under active national geochemical survey, and a candidate who can say so in one clause has converted a Cretaceous eruption into a contemporary statement without inventing anything.

❗ **Citation discipline.** The issuing body and the release date are cited. No press-release identification number is cited, because the official page could not be parsed directly in this environment and an unread identifier is not quoted.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Gondwana | Deccan Trap |
|---|---|---|
| Rock family | **Sedimentary** — continental, fluviatile, lacustrine | **Igneous** — extrusive flood basalt |
| Emplacement | Deposition in **subsiding loaded troughs** | **Fissure eruption** of extremely mobile basalt |
| Age | From the start of the **Permian, ~250 Ma** | End-**Cretaceous** to early **Eocene** |
| Thickness | **~6,000 m** of flat strata | Flows **0.5 m to 36 m**; province **150 m to ~3,000 m** |
| Extent | Four Peninsular belts plus Kashmir, Darjeeling, Sikkim | **~10 lakh sq km originally; ~5 lakh sq km now** |
| Economic signature | ✅ **~98 per cent of India's coal reserves** | Basalt → **regur** (🔁 Topic 04); building and road material |
| Classic error | Placed in the Dravidian | Called sedimentary; present extent read as original |

**Strongest objection.** The Deccan Trap is famous chiefly as a resource region — black soil, basalt aggregate, and now critical-mineral exploration. Is this lesson not really a resource lesson wearing a geological hat?

**Reply.** No, and the test is what the lesson actually asserts. It asserts a **mechanism** (fissure eruption of mobile basic lava), a **magnitude and its loss** (ten lakh to five lakh sq km), a **structure** (a west-thickening wedge in three divisions with inter-trappean beds proving episodic emplacement), and a **date bracket** (end-Cretaceous to early Eocene). None of those is a resource claim. ⚠️ Every resource claim that touches the province — its soil, its minerals, its exploration economics — is **named once and routed**. That boundary is the lesson's discipline, and Lesson 14 makes the discipline itself a teachable move.

⚠️ **Residual.** The threefold division, the thickness figures and the 98 per cent proportion are all **one author's account**; thicknesses in particular are summary values for a province with great internal variation. ❗ No production or reserve table is reproduced anywhere in this edition, and the administrative names used are the current ones.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Indian and World Geography — Physical" (Prelims, direct ownership); supports the GS-I physical-geography clause through the flood-basalt mechanism.
- **Verified linkage:** supports **2026 Prelims GS-I Q34** by supplying the Peninsula's defining Aryan sequence and its igneous episode. ❗ Provisional key; **answer-neutral**.
- 🔁 **Adjacent demands this lesson must not annex:** the 2022 GS-I question on the **natural-resource potential of the Deccan Trap** and the 2021 GS-I question on the **Gondwanaland mineral base and mining's share of output** are **resource-geography demands owned elsewhere**. This lesson supplies the lithology and stops. Lesson 14 teaches the restraint.
- **Probable framing:** in Mains, as the Indian instance in any question on flood basalts, fissure eruption or the lithological basis of India's fuel geography.
- **Traps:** the Trap called sedimentary; coal placed in the Deccan; present extent quoted as original; Gondwana placed in the Dravidian; Singareni placed in the Satpura basin; a Triassic thickness quoted; the 98 per cent figure presented as a current reserve statistic.
- **Answer use:** *"About ten lakh sq km of extremely mobile fissure-fed basalt covered the pre-existing topography between the close of the Cretaceous and the early Eocene; denudation has since halved it to about five lakh sq km."* That sentence contains the mechanism, the magnitude, the date bracket and the correction.

Revision notes:

- **Aryan nine-step spine:** Tethys geosyncline in the **Upper Carboniferous**, linked east to the Pacific via China and west to the Atlantic via Afghanistan, Iran, Asia Minor and the present Mediterranean → volcanic activity in the Kashmir Himalayas → **fissuring of Gondwanaland and India's northward drift** → **Deccan Trap** → **Arabian Sea and Bay of Bengal develop** → **Tertiary mountain-building raises the Himalayas** → India assumes its present shape → **Pleistocene Ice Age** → evolution and spread of man.
- ❗ **Pangaea assembled in the late Palaeozoic and broke up in the Mesozoic** — two events, opposite sides of the Permo-Triassic boundary.
- **Gondwana:** **continental, fluviatile and lacustrine** sediment in **geosynclinal troughs on an ancient plateau surface**; **loaded troughs subsided**, allowing **~6,000 m** of flat strata from the start of the **Permian, ~250 Ma**. ⚠️ The mechanism is a **shallow basin that kept sinking**, not a deep basin slowly filled.
- Name from the **Kingdom of the Gonds**; first discovered in the Gond region of Madhya Pradesh.
- **Four Peninsular belts:** **Damodar valley** (Jharkhand) · **Mahanadi valley** (Chhattisgarh and Odisha) · **southern Madhya Pradesh** · **troughs along the Godavari from Nagpur to the delta**. Extra-Peninsular: **Kashmir, Darjeeling, Sikkim**.
- **Lower Gondwana = Talcher + Panchet + Damuda.** **Talcher** from Talcher, Dhenkanal district, Odisha. **Damuda** carries the thicker eastern seams — **Raniganj, Jharia, Karanpura, Bokaro, Singrauli, Korba, Pench, Talcher, Singareni**. **Jhingurda seam ~131 m = thickest coal seam in India.** ❗ **Singareni is in the Godavari valley**, not the Satpura basin.
- ✅ **Gondwana rocks contain about 98 per cent of India's coal reserves** — a structural proportion, attributed, ❗ not a current reserve statistic.
- ❌ Coal in the Deccan; ❌ Gondwana in the Dravidian. **Tertiary coal is important in the north-east.**
- **Mesozoic marine:** **Triassic 280-225 Ma**, almost unknown in the Peninsula, exposed **Hazara to Nepal** (❗ no thickness quoted) · **Jurassic 180 Ma** (Jura mountains), **190 × 64 km** tract in **Kachchh**, **Jaisalmer** rocks · **Cretaceous 135-70 Ma** (*creta* = chalk), ✅ **"no other system is so widely distributed in India"**, **Bagh Beds** near Gwalior under the Trap, estuarine/lacustrine **Lametas**.
- **Deccan Trap:** end-**Cretaceous** to early **Eocene**; **~10 lakh sq km** of **extremely mobile fissure-fed basalt**, **covering fully the pre-existing topography**; **'trap'** from the **Swedish for stair/step**. ✅ **Spate prefers "Deccan Lava" to "Deccan Trap."**
- ❗ **Denudation has halved it: present extent ~5 lakh sq km.** Never quote the present figure as the eruption's extent.
- Flows **0.5 m to 36 m**. Thickness **~3,000 m Mumbai coast · 800 m Kachchh · 600-800 m southern limit · 150 m Amarkantak** (eastern limit) — a **west-thickening wedge**.
- **Upper Trap** Maharashtra and Saurashtra, many **inter-trappeans** and ash, ~**450 m** · **Middle Trap** Central India and Malwa, ~**1,200 m**, ash above, practically **devoid** of inter-trappeans · **Lower Trap** Central India and Tamil Nadu, ~**150 m**, inter-trappeans present, ash rare. **Inter-trappean beds prove episodic eruption** — sediment between successive flows.
- 📰 **GSI Field Season 2025-26 concluded** (PIB / Ministry of Mines, **2 April 2026**, GSI's **175th year**): **458** projects, **230** critical-mineral, **92** rare-earth, **80** reports for auction, **~22,000 sq km** geological mapping, **28,000 sq km geochemical mapping in the Deccan Traps**, **naturally occurring hydrogen in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands**, drone magnetic surveys and AI-driven targeting. 🔁 Project counts and auction reports **routed**, not developed.
- 🔁 Regur as a **soil** → Topic 04. Deccan **resource potential** and Gondwana **mining economics** → the resource owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how a continental, non-marine sedimentary sequence came to be about 6,000 m thick in the Gondwana basins, and say why that explanation also accounts for the thickness of their coal seams.

**Model answer:** Continental environments — rivers, lakes, swamps — are shallow, so they cannot accumulate six kilometres of sediment inside a static basin; the basin would fill and deposition would stop. The explanation is that the troughs were not static. Sediment was laid in geosynclinal troughs opened on an ancient plateau surface, and as it accumulated the loaded troughs subsided, so the floor went down at a rate comparable to the rate of infill. Deposition could therefore continue for a very long time without the environment ever changing or being drowned, which is why the sequences are both very thick and consistently freshwater and subaerial. The same mechanism accounts for the coal. A thick seam requires peat accumulation sustained over a long interval at nearly constant water level; continuing subsidence is exactly what maintains that condition, which is why these basins carry seams such as the Jhingurda at about 131 m.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not picture a deep pre-existing basin gradually filling up. The depth is a consequence of deposition, not a precondition for it; the load creates the accommodation space it then occupies.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The Deccan Trap as seen today is a residue, not a record." Examine this statement. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is largely sound. Between the close of the Cretaceous and the early Eocene, extremely mobile basalt issuing from fissures and cracks flooded about ten lakh sq km, covering the pre-existing topography entirely. Denudation has since reduced the province to about half, so the present extent of roughly five lakh sq km measures what survives, not what erupted. The surviving body is itself uneven: a west-thickening wedge of about 3,000 m on the Mumbai coast against only 150 m at Amarkantak, divided into Upper, Middle and Lower Traps. Yet "not a record" overstates it. The inter-trappean beds, sediments laid between successive flows, preserve the episodic rhythm of the eruption; the ash layers preserve its explosive phases; and the underlying Bagh Beds and Lametas date it from below. The province is therefore a reduced record, and the examiner's point is that extent must not be read as magnitude.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the ten-lakh to five-lakh correction stated with denudation as the cause; 2 for the fissure mechanism and the date bracket; 2 for the thickness wedge or the threefold division used as evidence of unevenness; 2 for the qualification that inter-trappeans, ash and underlying beds do preserve a record; 1 for the terminology of *trap*. Deduct 3 for quoting the present extent as the eruption's extent.

## Lesson 13 — The Aryan system II: Tertiary Himalaya, the Shiwaliks, and the Quaternary foredeep

Progress: 13 / 15  |  Stage: Advanced  |  Subtopic: Why the Tertiary is the pivot of India's geological history, how the Shiwaliks were built, and how the Great Plain became a structure

```text
      SECTION ACROSS THE FOREDEEP  -- the plain as a STRUCTURE

   stable Peninsular         THE GREAT PLAIN              rising HIMALAYA
   block (shield)            flat, featureless            still rising

                     the Himalayan FORELAND, of VARIABLE DEPTH, formed
                     CONCOMITANTLY with the rise of the Himalayas
   ====================================================================
   !! The plain "had not figured at all until the QUATERNARY".
      It is therefore the YOUNGEST of the three provinces, and it is a
      TECTONIC object -- a downwarp -- before it is a depositional one.

   AND THE STRATIGRAPHIC PAIR THAT SITS ON IT:
   BHANGAR  older alluvium  -> MIDDLE to UPPER PLEISTOCENE
   KHADAR   newer alluvium  -> UPPER PLEISTOCENE, occupying flood-prone
                               lower valley areas
   >> These are STRATIGRAPHIC UNITS defined by age and position.
      They are NOT soil types.
```

⚠️ Two visuals, both sequences: one in plan through time, one in section across space. The lesson's whole content is the conversion of an ocean into a mountain and of a mountain's load into a plain.

### Phase 1 — Why the Tertiary is the pivot

✅ **"The Tertiary is the most significant period in India's geological history because the Himalayas were born and India's present form came into being in this period."** ✅ It is called the **Age of the Mammals**.

⚠️ Three claims are folded into that sentence and each is separately examinable: the **mountain chain** was built; the country's **present outline** was fixed; and the **plain** that is India's demographic core did not yet exist. Everything a candidate associates with "India" as a physical object dates from this interval or later.

### Phase 2 — The three subsystems, in order

✅ **Eocene, about 60 Ma.** The belt runs from **Jammu to Shimla and Mussoorie**; it includes **Rajasthan lignite**, the coal-bearing sandstones of the **Jaintia series** of Meghalaya, and the **Barail series** of the Surma valley and the Naga Hills.

✅ **Oligocene to Lower Miocene, 40-25 Ma.** ✅ **Very poorly developed, because Tertiary outcrops suffered heavy denudation.** The **Murree series** of the Jammu hills forms a belt **25-40 km** wide, merging eastward into the **Dagshai-Kasauli (Sirmur) band** of about **2,600 m**.

✅ **The Shiwalik System, Middle Miocene to Lower Pleistocene, 14-0.2 Ma**, up to about **5,000 m** thick. ✅ Its **mammalian fossils were first discovered near Haridwar in 1839**, and ✅ its lower **Dupi Tila** beds **contain the oil fields of Lakhimpur and Digboi**.

✅ The Shiwaliks divide in three: **Upper 1,800-2,750 m**, **Middle 1,800-2,500 m**, **Lower 1,200-1,500 m**.

⚠️ **The Shiwaliks are the mountain's own debris.** They are the sediment eroded off a rising range and dumped at its foot, then caught up and folded as the deformation front advanced southward. That is why they are young, thick, coarse and fossil-rich — and it is why they carry hydrocarbons, because a thick, rapidly buried sedimentary pile is exactly the setting a petroleum system requires. 🔁 The **distribution and economics of that petroleum** belong to the resource owner; what this lesson supplies is the structural reason it is there.

### Phase 3 — Three dated phases of upheaval, with their source attached

✅ The Himalayan rise occurred in **three phases**:

| Phase | Date | What it produced |
|---|---|---|
| First | **Eocene, about 65 Ma**, culminating in the Oligocene | The **Greater Himalayas** |
| Second | **Mid-Miocene, about 45 Ma** | Folding of the **Lesser Himalayas** |
| Third | **Post-Pliocene, about 1.4 Ma** | Folding of the **Shiwaliks** |

✅ **And the Himalayas are still rising.**

⚠️ Read the table as a **southward-advancing deformation front**: the oldest phase built the northernmost and highest ranges, and each later phase folded material lying further south and younger. That is why the Shiwaliks — the youngest, outermost and lowest range — were folded last, out of sediment the earlier phases had themselves produced.

❗ **These dates are quoted with their source attached**, as both canonical owners require for any collision or orogenic date. ⚠️ Outside this attribution, no collision date appears in this file.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ "The Himalaya is volcanic in origin." ✅ It is a **young fold-and-thrust mountain system containing sedimentary, metamorphic and crystalline units**, raised by **continent-continent collision**, which is seismically very active and ❗ **volcanically quiet** (Lesson 8). ⚠️ Two further correctives travel with it: the Himalaya does **not** consist only of young Tertiary sedimentary strata — Archaean rocks form the backbone of the system (Lesson 10) — and **Mount Everest is composed of Upper Carboniferous limestones** (Lesson 11), which is marine rock, not igneous rock.

### Phase 4 — The Quaternary, and the plain as a structure

✅ **The Great Indo-Gangetic-Brahmaputra Plain had not figured at all until the Quaternary.** ✅ Its bottom configuration is a **synclinal basin called the foredeep** — a **downwarp of the Himalayan foreland, of variable depth, formed concomitantly with the rise of the Himalayas**.

⚠️ **This is the claim that makes the plain a geological object rather than a depositional accident.** A plain that is simply "where sediment collected" invites no further question. A plain that is a **downwarp formed concomitantly with the mountains** is part of the orogenic system: the load of the rising range depressed the crust in front of it, and that depression was then filled by the range's own erosion products. ❗ An answer that describes the Indo-Gangetic plain merely as an alluvial surface has missed the structure beneath it.

✅ **The Pleistocene** is marked by cold climate and glaciation. ✅ **The Recent began about 12,000 years ago**, with the withdrawal of the last glaciation. ✅ **De Terra and Paterson** divided the age into **four glacial and three inter-glacial periods**, and ✅ sea level stood about **100 m lower** during glacials.

✅ **Karewas** are **flat-topped terraces of clays, sands and silts with lenticles of old deltaic-fan conglomerate** on the flanks of the **Pir Panjal**, assigned to the **second inter-glacial**.

✅ **The stratigraphic pair that is most often mis-taught:** the **older alluvium, *bhangar*, is of Middle-to-Upper Pleistocene age**; the **newer alluvium, *khadar*, occupying flood-prone lower valley areas, is Upper Pleistocene**.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ Treating *bhangar* and *khadar* as **soil types**. ✅ They are **alluvial stratigraphic units defined by age and position** — older and higher against newer and flood-prone. ⚠️ They have soil consequences, and those consequences belong to the soils owner; the units themselves belong here, in the Quaternary.

### Phase 5 — Three Indian ice ages, as stratigraphic evidence only

✅ India's glacial record is given as three named episodes:

1. ✅ **The Dharwar Ice Age**, evidenced by **conglomerates near Kaldurg** in South India.
2. ✅ **The Gondwana Ice Age**, proved by the **Talchir series** in Odisha, whose **boulders match those of Shimla, Hazara and the Salt Range** — the drift evidence of Lesson 6.
3. ✅ **The Pleistocene Ice Age** in the Himalayas, with **glaciation down to 1,800 m** and **glacial drift and terminal moraines down to 1,400 m in Kashmir**; **ancient moraines before the snouts of existing glaciers at about 1,650 m**; and glacial lakes including **Kailash Kund, Gulmarg, Sheshnag and Kaunsarnag**.

✅ **The Peninsular part of India shows no evidence of glaciation during the Pleistocene.**

⚠️ That negative is the useful half. India's tropical latitudes were not glaciated in the Pleistocene, yet tropical India carries a **Gondwana** glacial record — which is only possible if the block was somewhere else when that record was laid down. ⚠️ The two statements together are a complete drift argument in two lines.

🔁 **Glacial landforms as landforms** — moraine morphology, cirques, U-valleys, periglacial features — belong to the glacial-landforms owner. This lesson takes the ice-age record **strictly as stratigraphic evidence** of past climate and past position. ⚠️ Lake names are quoted only for those that are legible without ambiguity in the source.

### Phase 6 — The coastline as a structural fact

✅ India has about **6,100 km** of coast, mostly uniform and regular. ✅ **Suess attributes its straightness and regularity to the disruption and faulting of Gondwanaland during the Cretaceous**, and ✅ **as such the coast of India does not offer many sites for good natural harbours.** ✅ **The Bay of Bengal and the Arabian Sea came into being during the Cretaceous or early Tertiary after the disintegration of Gondwanaland.**

❗ **A boundary that must be respected.** The **6,100 km** figure is quoted here **strictly as a book-era structural statement** supporting the straightness argument. 🔁 **The measurement of India's coastline, and the convention by which that measurement is made, belong to Topic 01**, where the question of method, datum, scale and inclusions is properly treated. ❗ This edition does not restate or revise that convention and does not use the figure as a current measurement.

✅ **Evidence of emergence and submergence** follows, and it is the part that earns marks:

| Evidence | What it indicates |
|---|---|
| ✅ A **submerged forest 6-12 m below sea level** on the eastern side of **Mumbai island** | Submergence |
| ✅ A **lignite bed 73 m below ground at Puducherry** | Submergence |
| ✅ **Submerged Dwarka** | Submergence |
| ✅ The **1819 Rann of Kachchh earthquake**, which submerged **5,180 sq km** including **Sindree fort** and simultaneously **elevated about 1,550 sq km** | Both, in one event |
| ✅ **Raised beaches at 30-45 m on the Makran coast** | Emergence |
| ✅ The **steep face of the Sahyadri**, read as evidence of a **recent elevation of the Ghats from the sea** | Emergence |

⚠️ The Kachchh case is the best of them because it carries both signs in a single dated event, which demonstrates that emergence and submergence are **not alternatives** but simultaneous consequences of one deformation.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Tertiary (L13 first half) | Quaternary (L13 second half) |
|---|---|---|
| What is built | The **Himalaya**; India's present form | The **Great Plain**; the present surface |
| Age bracket | ~**65 Ma** to ~**1.4 Ma**, in three phases | Pleistocene to the **Recent, from ~12,000 years ago** |
| Characteristic deposit | **Shiwalik molasse**, up to ~**5,000 m** | **Alluvium** — ***bhangar*** and ***khadar*** |
| Structural object | A fold-and-thrust belt, **still rising** | A **foredeep**, subsiding and filling |
| Climatic signature | Age of the **Mammals** | ✅ **Four glacial and three inter-glacial** periods; sea level ~**100 m lower** in glacials |
| Resource signature | 🔁 Petroleum in the Shiwalik Dupi Tila beds — **Lakhimpur and Digboi**; Tertiary coal in the north-east | Groundwater in the alluvium — 🔁 Topic 04 |

**Strongest objection.** The Himalaya has a physiographic owner elsewhere in this folder. Does this lesson not trespass?

**Reply.** It takes three things and leaves the rest. It takes the **three dated upheaval phases**, because they are chronology and chronology is this topic's; it takes the **Shiwalik stratigraphy**, because it is a rock system in the ladder; and it takes the **foredeep**, because the foredeep is a structure rather than a landform. 🔁 What it leaves alone is the entire physiographic sub-division of the Himalaya — the longitudinal divisions, the regional segments, the passes, the valleys — which belongs to the physiography owner and does not appear in this file at all.

⚠️ **Residual.** The Pleistocene glacial-interglacial framework quoted here is **de Terra and Paterson's**, an older scheme that later work has refined; the Shiwalik thickness figures are summary values; and the glacial-lake list is given only to the extent that it reads unambiguously in the source. ❗ Each is attributed rather than asserted.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Indian and World Geography — Physical" (Prelims, direct ownership); supports the GS-I clause through the collision-orogen and foreland-basin mechanism.
- **Verified linkage:** supports **2026 Prelims GS-I Q34** by supplying the extra-Peninsular contrast term against which the Peninsular Block is defined. ❗ Provisional key; **answer-neutral**.
- **Probable framing:** in Mains, as the chronological spine of any answer on the origin of the Himalaya and the Northern Plain, and as the evidence paragraph in any question on changing sea level or coastal emergence.
- **Traps:** the Himalaya called volcanic; the Himalaya described as only young sedimentary rock; *bhangar* and *khadar* called soils; the plain treated as a depositional surface with no structure beneath it; a collision date quoted without its source; the 6,100 km coastline used as a current measurement rather than as a book-era structural statement.
- **Answer use:** *"The foredeep is a downwarp of the Himalayan foreland formed concomitantly with the rise of the range, and the plain is its fill."* That one sentence converts a description of the Northern Plain into an explanation of it, and it is the single highest-yield line in this lesson.

Revision notes:

- ✅ **"The Tertiary is the most significant period in India's geological history because the Himalayas were born and India's present form came into being in this period."** Called the **Age of the Mammals**.
- **Eocene ~60 Ma:** Jammu belt to **Shimla and Mussoorie**; **Rajasthan lignite**; **Jaintia series** coal-bearing sandstones of Meghalaya; **Barail series** of the Surma valley and Naga Hills.
- **Oligocene-Lower Miocene 40-25 Ma:** **very poorly developed because Tertiary outcrops suffered heavy denudation**; **Murree series** of the Jammu hills, a **25-40 km** belt, merging east into the **Dagshai-Kasauli (Sirmur)** band of ~**2,600 m**.
- **Shiwalik System, Middle Miocene to Lower Pleistocene, 14-0.2 Ma**, up to ~**5,000 m**; **mammalian fossils first discovered near Haridwar in 1839**; lower **Dupi Tila** beds contain the oil fields of **Lakhimpur and Digboi**. Divisions: **Upper 1,800-2,750 m · Middle 1,800-2,500 m · Lower 1,200-1,500 m**.
- ⚠️ The Shiwaliks are the **mountain's own debris**, later caught up and folded by a southward-advancing front.
- **Three upheaval phases (source-attached):** **Eocene ~65 Ma**, culminating in the Oligocene → **Greater Himalayas**; **mid-Miocene ~45 Ma** → **Lesser Himalayas**; **post-Pliocene ~1.4 Ma** → **Shiwaliks**. ✅ **Still rising.**
- ❌ Himalaya volcanic; ❌ Himalaya as only young Tertiary sedimentary rock. It is a **fold-and-thrust belt of sedimentary, metamorphic and crystalline units**, seismically active and **volcanically quiet**.
- ✅ **The Great Indo-Gangetic-Brahmaputra Plain had not figured at all until the Quaternary**; its floor is a **synclinal basin, the foredeep — a downwarp of the Himalayan foreland of variable depth, formed concomitantly with the rise of the Himalayas**.
- **Quaternary:** Pleistocene cold and glaciated; **the Recent began about 12,000 years ago**; **de Terra and Paterson** — **four glacial, three inter-glacial**; sea level ~**100 m lower** in glacials.
- **Karewas:** flat-topped terraces of clays, sands and silts with lenticles of old deltaic-fan conglomerate on the flanks of the **Pir Panjal**; **second inter-glacial**.
- ❗ ***Bhangar*** = older alluvium, **Middle-to-Upper Pleistocene**; ***khadar*** = newer alluvium, **Upper Pleistocene**, flood-prone lower valley areas. **Stratigraphic units, not soils.**
- **Three ice ages:** **Dharwar** — conglomerates near **Kaldurg** · **Gondwana** — **Talchir series**, Odisha, boulders matching **Shimla, Hazara, the Salt Range** · **Pleistocene** — Himalayan glaciation to **1,800 m**, drift and terminal moraines to **1,400 m** in Kashmir, ancient moraines at ~**1,650 m**, lakes including **Kailash Kund, Gulmarg, Sheshnag, Kaunsarnag**. ✅ **The Peninsula shows no Pleistocene glaciation.**
- **Coastline as structure:** about **6,100 km**, uniform and regular; **Suess** attributes the straightness to the **disruption and faulting of Gondwanaland during the Cretaceous**; **few sites for good natural harbours**. 🔁 Coastline **measurement and convention** → Topic 01.
- **Emergence and submergence:** submerged forest **6-12 m** below sea level east of **Mumbai island** · lignite bed **73 m** below ground at **Puducherry** · submerged **Dwarka** · the **1819 Rann of Kachchh earthquake**, submerging **5,180 sq km** including **Sindree fort** and elevating about **1,550 sq km** · raised beaches **30-45 m** on the **Makran** coast · the steep **Sahyadri** face as evidence of recent elevation.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it more accurate to describe the Indo-Gangetic plain as a tectonic object than as a depositional one, and what does that reclassification allow an answer to explain that the depositional description cannot?

**Model answer:** The plain's floor is a synclinal basin — a foredeep — which is a downwarp of the Himalayan foreland of variable depth, formed concomitantly with the rise of the Himalayas. The depression therefore exists for a tectonic reason: the load of the rising range depressed the crust in front of it. Alluvium is what subsequently filled that depression, so deposition is the consequence and the downwarp is the cause. The reclassification buys three explanations. It explains the plain's extraordinary depth of fill, which no surface process could produce on a stable platform. It explains why the plain did not figure at all until the Quaternary, since it could not exist before the range that created it. And it explains the plain's position and elongation, lying between a rising belt and a rigid block rather than anywhere sediment happened to accumulate. A depositional description leaves all three unexplained.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the plain's flatness as evidence that nothing tectonic is involved. Flatness is a surface property of the fill; the structure is underneath it, and the two are independent.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "India's coastline is structurally determined, and its present form records both emergence and submergence." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The structural claim rests on origin. Suess attributed the straightness and regularity of the Indian coast to the disruption and faulting of Gondwanaland during the Cretaceous, and the Arabian Sea and Bay of Bengal themselves came into being in the Cretaceous or early Tertiary after that disintegration. A faulted margin yields a straight coast, which is why the coast offers few sites for good natural harbours — a structural inheritance with an economic consequence. Movement since then has gone both ways. Submergence is recorded by a drowned forest six to twelve metres below sea level east of Mumbai island, a lignite bed seventy-three metres below ground at Puducherry, and submerged Dwarka. Emergence is recorded by raised beaches at thirty to forty-five metres on the Makran coast and the steep Sahyadri face. The 1819 Rann of Kachchh earthquake did both, submerging 5,180 sq km while elevating about 1,550 sq km.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the Gondwana-faulting origin attributed to Suess with the harbour consequence drawn; 3 for at least two submergence and two emergence cases with their measurements; 2 for the 1819 Kachchh event used as a single bidirectional case; 1 for the Cretaceous-to-early-Tertiary origin of the two seas; 1 for restraint on the coastline-length convention. Deduct 2 for treating the coastline figure as a current measurement.

## Lesson 14 — Structure, relief and resource: the causal fan, the hill-range inventory, and the limits of the argument

Progress: 14 / 15  |  Stage: Advanced  |  Subtopic: How lithology fans out into relief, water, soil, resource and settlement — and where that chain stops being an explanation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9.2-§9.3 (the six divisions and what each determines; why the division set explains so much; the factual caution); D.R. Khullar, Chapter 2, printed p.40 (why geological structure matters; the paired alluvium-against-shield contrast) and Chapter 3, printed pp.82-85 (the Peninsular plateau sub-divisions and the hill-range inventory).
CA search: "Eastern Ghats continuity Aravalli hill ranges Amarkantak Seshachalam 2026 news" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in last 6 months on hill-range structure. The file's one current linkage, carried at Lesson 12, is referenced here once in the closing causal-fan paragraph and is not re-developed.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
                        THE CAUSAL FAN
   =====================================================================
                        G E O L O G I C A L
                        S T R U C T U R E
                               |
         +---------+-----------+-----------+-----------+
         |         |           |           |           |
         v         v           v           v           v
      RELIEF    DRAINAGE    GROUNDWATER   SOIL      MINERAL
      plateau,  pattern     quantity      parent    ASSOCIATION
      scarp,    follows     AND quality   material  shield -> metals
      rift,     structure   fracture vs             Gondwana -> coal
      plain                 pore storage            Tertiary -> oil
         |         |           |           |           |
         +---------+-----+-----+-----------+-----------+
                         |
                         v
              LAND USE | IRRIGATION POTENTIAL | TRANSPORT AND
              COMMUNICATION ALIGNMENT | SETTLEMENT DENSITY |
              UNDERSTANDING OF EARTHQUAKES, VOLCANOES,
              LANDSLIDES AND FLOODS
                         |
                         v
   ==================== !! WHERE THE FAN STOPS !! =====================
   The fan yields ASSOCIATION, never DETERMINATION. At every node,
   climate, relief, time, organisms, technology, price and policy act
   alongside the rock. Structure sets a PROBABILITY DISTRIBUTION over
   outcomes; it does not select one.
   =====================================================================
   THE PAIRED CONTRAST THAT PROVES THE FAN IS REAL:
   the alluvial Indo-Gangetic plain is EXTREMELY USEFUL FOR
   AGRICULTURE but ALMOST COMPLETELY DEVOID OF MINERAL DEPOSITS;
   the igneous/metamorphic Peninsular plateau, ESPECIALLY
   CHOTANAGPUR, is VERY RICH in minerals.
   Two adjacent regions; opposite endowments; one geological cause.
```

⚠️ A fan, not a chain — because structure does not produce one consequence in sequence but several in parallel, which then interact. ⚠️ And the warning band at the bottom is part of the visual, not an afterthought: it is what prevents the fan from becoming determinism.

### Branch 1 — Why geological structure matters, stated by the source

✅ Khullar's own list of why geological structure is studied is the fan in prose: **land-use planning, irrigation potential, groundwater quality and quantity, transport and communication alignment, and the understanding of earthquakes, volcanoes, landslides and floods.**

✅ And the paired contrast that proves the point: the **alluvial Indo-Gangetic plain is extremely useful for agriculture but almost completely devoid of mineral deposits**, while the **igneous and metamorphic Peninsular plateau, especially Chotanagpur, is very rich**.

⚠️ That pairing is the best single piece of evidence in the India half, because it is a **controlled comparison**. Two adjacent regions of one country, under broadly similar national policy, with opposite resource endowments — and the variable that differs is the rock. ⚠️ Deploy it whenever a question asks why India's mineral and agricultural geographies do not coincide.

### Branch 2 — The six divisions, and what each determines

⚠️ The Core owner's six-division table is the systematic expression of the fan. It is reproduced here in compressed form because it is the structure-to-land-use chain the syllabus clause actually wants.

| Division | Structural character | Relief and drainage consequence | Land-use and settlement consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Himalaya and associated ranges** | Young fold mountains in parallel longitudinal ranges, cut by deep transverse gorges | Snow and glacier storage feeding **perennial** rivers; extreme relief; high seismic and mass-movement hazard; heavy orographic rainfall on the southern face | Terraced and horticultural agriculture; vertical zonation; costly transport; hydropower with hazard exposure |
| **Northern (Indo-Gangetic-Brahmaputra) Plain** | Deep alluvial fill in a **foredeep** | Extremely low gradient; meandering, shifting channels; **khadar and bhangar** surfaces; productive shallow aquifers | India's densest settlement and most intensive cultivation; flood exposure; groundwater dependence |
| **Peninsular Plateau** | Ancient shield with block-faulted uplands, escarpments and rift valleys; extensive lava cover in the north-west | Moderate relief; **seasonal** rivers on hard rock with little alluvium; limited groundwater in crystalline terrain but useful **fracture** storage | Mineral wealth in the shield; rain-fed and drought-prone farming; black-soil cotton belt on the lava tract |
| **Western and Eastern Ghats** | Faulted escarpment on the west; discontinuous, dissected hills on the east | The Western Ghats form a near-continuous **orographic barrier** and the Peninsula's water divide; the eastern hills are breached by large rivers | Heavy windward rainfall and plantation crops in the west; rain-shadow drylands to the east |
| **Coastal Plains** | Narrow on the west, broad on the east | West: **short, swift, estuary-forming** streams; east: wide and **deltaic**, built by large peninsular rivers | Fishing, ports and backwater economy in the west; deltaic rice and dense settlement in the east, with cyclone and surge exposure |
| **The Islands** | A submarine ridge and arc group in the Bay of Bengal; coral-built islands in the Arabian Sea | Volcanic and tectonically active in the east; low and reef-derived in the west | Contrasting vulnerability: seismic and tsunami exposure against sea-level and freshwater-lens fragility |

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ Treating the two Ghats as a matched pair. ✅ The **Western Ghats are a continuous faulted escarpment** forming the Peninsula's water divide; the **Eastern Ghats are discontinuous, lower and repeatedly cut through by rivers**. ⚠️ That asymmetry explains the entire drainage pattern of Peninsular India — 🔁 the drainage pattern itself being the fluvial owner's subject, supplied here only with its structural cause.

### Branch 3 — The hill-range inventory, which carries an owned demand

🔗 **Linked demand (answer-neutral):** **2023 Prelims GS-I Q9 — Indian hill ranges, naming Amarkantak, Biligirirangan and Seshachalam.** ❗ The official key is **unavailable locally**; no answer is recorded or inferred. What this lesson owes the demand is the **structural identity of the Peninsular hill systems**, each typed by mechanism rather than by height.

**The Aravali.** ✅ The **relict of the world's oldest mountain, formed as a result of folding at the close of the Archaean era**; running **800 km from Delhi to Palanpur**; **Guru Sikhar 1,722 m** and **Mt Abu 1,158 m**; its north-eastern end marked by the **Delhi ridge** and continuing toward Haridwar **buried under alluvium**; ✅ **Chatterjee assigns it an Algonkian age of 600-700 Ma**.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ Treating the Aravali as a currently high mountain. It is a **relict of the oldest orogeny**; its low elevation is the *evidence* for its age, not an embarrassment to it.

**The Satpura.** ✅ A **horst** — *sat* + *pura*, "seven mountains" — with **Dhupgarh (1,350 m) on the Mahadev Hills** as its highest point, plus **Astamba Dongar (1,325 m)** and **Amarkantak (1,127 m)**. ⚠️ Mechanism: **tension and bounding faults**, as at Lesson 5.

**The Western Ghats / Sahyadri.** ✅ Running **1,600 km from the Tapi valley to Kanniyakumari**; **Anai Mudi 2,695 m**, **Doda Betta 2,637 m**, **Makurti 2,554 m**; and the **Palghat Gap, 24-30 km wide at 75-300 m, in all probability a rift valley formed by subsidence between two parallel fault lines.**

**The Eastern Ghats, and the nomenclature contest.** ✅ Described as **"a chain of highly broken and detached hills"** running from the **Mahanadi to the Vaigai**, having **neither structural unity nor physiographic continuity**, so that ✅ **the name is a misnomer**. ✅ **Spate preferred "Eastern Hills"**, and ✅ many geographers use **Eastern Ghats** for the northern section, **Cuddapah Ranges** for the centre and **Tamilnad Hills** for the south, with ✅ **Mahendra Giri (1,501 m)** as the tallest peak.

⚠️ **Use the contest.** This is the kind of qualification that separates a 12 from a 15: stating that a familiar name is contested, naming the author who contested it and naming the alternative nomenclature demonstrates that the candidate knows the difference between a label and a structure.

> 🔑 **Trap.** ❌ Describing the Eastern Ghats as a continuous range. ✅ They are **highly broken and detached**, with **neither structural unity nor physiographic continuity**, and are repeatedly breached by the large east-flowing rivers.

### Branch 4 — The plateau sub-divisions, compressed

✅ The Peninsular plateau's sub-divisions, with the measurements that matter:

- ✅ **Malwa** — two drainage systems; the ***Pat*** lands as a high-level laterite plateau.
- ✅ **Baghelkhand**.
- ✅ **Chotanagpur** — **over 87,000 sq km**; the **Hazaribagh Plateau** with the *Gorus* at **1,119 m and 1,142 m**; the **Ranchi plateau** at **600 m**; **Parasnath 1,366 m**.
- ✅ **Meghalaya** — **Garo 900 m, Khasi-Jaintia 1,500 m, Mikir 700 m**; **Shillong 1,961 m**, the highest point.
- ✅ **The Deccan** — about **five lakh sq km**, sub-divided into the **Maharashtra, Karnataka** (*Malnad* and *Maidan*) and **Telangana** plateaus.
- ✅ **The Chhattisgarh Plain** — a **saucer-shaped depression on nearly horizontal Cuddapah limestone and shale**, described as **"the only plain worth the name in the vast stretch of plateaus and hill ranges of the Peninsular plateau."**

⚠️ The Chhattisgarh case is the fan in miniature: a **near-horizontal Purana sedimentary floor** inside a crystalline upland produces the only genuine plain in the Peninsula, and therefore the only Peninsular region with plains agriculture at scale. 🇮🇳 One lithological fact, one landform, one agricultural geography.

### Branch 5 — Where the fan stops, and the restraint that is itself a teachable move

⚠️ Three limits must travel with every use of the fan.

1. **Association, not determination.** ✅ Geological association supports a **probability**, not a deterministic mineral or hazard prediction. ⚠️ A shield is a favourable host for metallic ores; it does not guarantee a deposit, and grade, depth, accessibility, price and policy decide what is mined.
2. **Multiple causation at every node.** ✅ Soil formation depends on climate, relief, organisms and time as well as on parent rock, so **parent rock alone does not determine soil**. The same is true of drainage, of groundwater and of settlement.
3. **Numbers are the weak point.** ❗ ✅ **Do not quote peak heights, plain widths, plateau areas, coastline lengths or the number of physiographic sub-divisions from memory.** Different textbooks divide the Peninsula differently. ⚠️ Every figure in Branch 3 and Branch 4 above is given **with its source**, and outside that attribution the rule is to describe the structural logic and the consequence rather than a contested count.

🔁 **And the restraint on ownership, which is the move this lesson exists to teach.** Three previous-year demands are *tempting* for this topic because their lithology is this topic's lithology — the **2022 GS-I question on the natural-resource potential of the Deccan Trap**, the **2021 GS-I question on the Gondwanaland mineral base against mining's share of output**, and the **2023 Prelims question on carbon capture by basalt and lime injection**. ❗ **All three are owned elsewhere**: the first two by the mineral-and-energy-resources topic, the third by the environment folder. ⚠️ The correct move is to **name the substrate, state the route, and stop** — and a candidate who does that in a real answer has demonstrated syllabus control rather than ignorance.

📰 One permitted contemporary note closes the fan: the Deccan Traps are currently the subject of **28,000 sq km of geochemical mapping** under the Geological Survey of India's Field Season 2025-26, which is the clearest available demonstration that the structure-to-resource chain is an **operational** proposition and not only a textbook one. 🔁 Its exploration and auction dimensions remain routed.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Indo-Gangetic Plain | Peninsular Plateau |
|---|---|---|
| Lithology | Quaternary **alluvium** over a foredeep | **Archaean-Dharwar crystallines**, Purana basins, Gondwana troughs, Deccan basalt |
| Agriculture | ✅ **Extremely useful** | Rain-fed, drought-prone; black-soil cotton on the lava tract |
| Minerals | ✅ **Almost completely devoid** | ✅ **Very rich, especially Chotanagpur** |
| Water | Productive shallow aquifers | Limited primary storage; **fracture** storage in crystallines |
| Rivers | Perennial, snow-fed, low-gradient, shifting | **Seasonal**, on hard rock, little alluvium |
| Settlement | Densest in India | Sparser rural density |
| Single cause of the contrast | **The rock** | **The rock** |

**Strongest objection.** The causal fan looks like environmental determinism in modern dress. Does it not simply assert that geology decides how Indians live?

**Reply.** It does not, and the structure of the argument shows why. The fan claims that structure **sets the distribution of possibilities** at each node and that the possibilities narrow as one moves outward — rock to relief is tight, relief to drainage is tight, drainage to settlement is loose, and settlement to prosperity is looser still. ⚠️ At every node a second set of variables enters: climate and time at the soil node, technology and capital at the resource node, price and policy at the land-use node. **The Gangetic plain is not densely settled because it is alluvial; it is densely settled because alluvium plus monsoon water plus a long agrarian history plus modern irrigation made it so.** The honest formulation is that structure explains the **pattern of opportunity**, and the social and economic variables explain what was done with it.

⚠️ **Residual.** The six-division scheme is itself one scheme among several, and the Core owner says so: different textbooks divide the Peninsula differently and the sub-division count is contested. ❗ Describe the logic; do not defend a count.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Indian and World Geography — Physical" (Prelims, direct ownership); "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains GS-I) where the structure-to-relief chain is generalised. 🔁 The resource distribution itself is the mineral-and-energy owner's; disaster governance is the disaster owner's.
- **Verified linkage:** **2023 Prelims GS-I Q9 — Indian hill ranges (Amarkantak, Biligirirangan, Seshachalam).** ❗ Key unavailable locally; **answer-neutral**. The owed content is the **structural typing of Peninsular hill systems** — fold relict, horst, faulted escarpment, detached hill chain — and the recognition that **Amarkantak** appears in this topic twice, as the eastern limit of the Deccan Trap at 150 m thickness and as a Satpura summit at 1,127 m.
- **Probable framing:** in Mains, as the explanatory engine of any question on uneven population density, uneven mineral distribution, Deccan drought-proneness or the contrast between India's two coasts.
- **Traps:** Eastern Ghats called continuous; Aravali treated as currently high; the two Ghats paired as equivalents; peak heights or plateau areas quoted from memory; the fan read as determinism; resource-economy demands annexed instead of routed; book-era production figures quoted as current.
- **Answer use:** the controlled comparison is the strongest twenty words available — *"the alluvial plain is agriculturally rich and almost devoid of minerals; the crystalline plateau is the reverse; the variable is the rock."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Why structure matters:** land-use planning · irrigation potential · groundwater quality and quantity · transport and communication alignment · understanding of earthquakes, volcanoes, landslides and floods.
- ✅ **The controlled contrast:** the alluvial Indo-Gangetic plain is **extremely useful for agriculture but almost completely devoid of mineral deposits**; the Peninsular plateau, **especially Chotanagpur**, is **very rich**.
- **Six divisions:** Himalaya · Northern Plain (foredeep fill) · Peninsular Plateau · the two Ghats · Coastal Plains · Islands — each with its structural character, relief/drainage consequence and land-use consequence.
- ❌ **The two Ghats are not a matched pair.** Western = continuous **faulted escarpment**, the Peninsula's **water divide**. Eastern = **discontinuous, lower, repeatedly breached**.
- 🇮🇳 **Aravali:** relict of the **world's oldest mountain**, folded at the **close of the Archaean**; **800 km, Delhi to Palanpur**; **Guru Sikhar 1,722 m**, **Mt Abu 1,158 m**; **Delhi ridge** at the north-eastern end, continuing to Haridwar **under alluvium**; **Chatterjee: Algonkian, 600-700 Ma**.
- 🇮🇳 **Satpura = horst** (*sat* + *pura*); **Dhupgarh 1,350 m** on the **Mahadev Hills**; **Astamba Dongar 1,325 m**; **Amarkantak 1,127 m**.
- 🇮🇳 **Western Ghats / Sahyadri:** **1,600 km**, Tapi valley to Kanniyakumari; **Anai Mudi 2,695 m**, **Doda Betta 2,637 m**, **Makurti 2,554 m**; **Palghat Gap 24-30 km wide at 75-300 m**, in all probability a **rift valley between two parallel faults**.
- 🇮🇳 **Eastern Ghats:** **"a chain of highly broken and detached hills"**, Mahanadi to the Vaigai, with **neither structural unity nor physiographic continuity**; ✅ **the name is a misnomer**; **Spate preferred "Eastern Hills"**; many use **Eastern Ghats / Cuddapah Ranges / Tamilnad Hills** for north, centre and south; **Mahendra Giri 1,501 m** tallest.
- **Plateau sub-divisions:** **Malwa** (two drainage systems; ***Pat*** high-level laterite) · **Baghelkhand** · **Chotanagpur** (**over 87,000 sq km**; **Hazaribagh** *Gorus* **1,119 m and 1,142 m**; **Ranchi 600 m**; **Parasnath 1,366 m**) · **Meghalaya** (**Garo 900 m, Khasi-Jaintia 1,500 m, Mikir 700 m**; **Shillong 1,961 m**) · **Deccan** (~**five lakh sq km**: Maharashtra, Karnataka with ***Malnad*** and ***Maidan***, Telangana) · **Chhattisgarh Plain**, a **saucer-shaped depression on nearly horizontal Cuddapah limestone and shale**, ✅ **"the only plain worth the name"** in the Peninsula.
- ❗ **Three limits:** association not determination · multiple causation at every node · ❗ **no peak heights, plain widths, plateau areas, coastline lengths or sub-division counts from memory**.
- 🔁 **Route, do not annex:** Deccan resource potential and Gondwanaland mining economics → the mineral-and-energy owner; basalt carbon capture → the environment owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate argues that India's mineral poverty in the north and mineral wealth in the east-central Peninsula "proves that geology determines economic geography". Assess the argument and restate it correctly.

**Model answer:** The evidence is real and the inference is overstated. The alluvial Indo-Gangetic plain is extremely useful for agriculture but almost completely devoid of mineral deposits, while the igneous and metamorphic Peninsular plateau, especially Chotanagpur, is very rich — two adjacent regions, opposite endowments, and the differing variable is the rock. What the comparison establishes is that **where metallic minerals can occur at all** is fixed by lithology: a Quaternary alluvial fill cannot host ores that form in crystalline basement. What it does not establish is economic outcome. Geological association supports a probability, not a prediction: outcrop indicates a favourable host, while grade, depth, accessibility, price, infrastructure and policy decide what is extracted, and the plain's agricultural wealth shows that mineral absence is not economic absence. The correct restatement is that structure fixes the pattern of opportunity, and social, technological and policy variables determine what is made of it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not infer from a strong spatial correlation that no other variable is operating. The correlation holds at the node where it is tightest — rock to possible mineral host — and weakens at every node further out along the fan.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The Eastern Ghats are a misnomer." Examine this claim and state what the naming dispute reveals about describing relief. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is well founded. The Eastern Ghats are described as a chain of highly broken and detached hills running from the Mahanadi to the Vaigai, having neither structural unity nor physiographic continuity, and repeatedly breached by the large east-flowing rivers. "Ghats" implies a continuous escarpment, accurate for the Sahyadri — a faulted, near-continuous wall forming the Peninsula's water divide — and inaccurate here. Spate preferred "Eastern Hills", and many geographers divide the system into the Eastern Ghats in the north, the Cuddapah Ranges in the centre and the Tamilnad Hills in the south, with Mahendra Giri at 1,501 m the tallest peak. The dispute reveals a discipline: a relief name is a hypothesis about structure, and a name that imports continuity where none exists will carry that error into drainage, rainfall and land-use reasoning. The qualification is that the name is entrenched in official usage and cannot be abandoned.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the structural grounds — broken, detached, no structural unity or physiographic continuity, breached by rivers; 2 for the contrast with the Western Ghats as a continuous faulted escarpment and water divide; 2 for the alternative nomenclature with Spate named; 2 for the general point that a relief name encodes a structural hypothesis; 1 for the entrenchment qualification. Deduct 2 for treating the two Ghats as equivalents.

```text
   THE PLOTTING RAIL -- build the map, never memorise the finished one
   ===================================================================
   STEP 0   OUTLINE: coast, the two great river systems, state frames
            only as needed. Draw dispute-safe; do not sharpen a
            boundary the map does not settle.
                 |
   STEP 1   SHIELD AND GRANITES  >> unclassified crystallines,
            granites of all ages, CHARNOCKITES. These go down FIRST
            because everything else sits on them.      [L10]
                 |
   STEP 2   DHARWAR SYSTEM       >> belts WITHIN the shield, not a
            blanket: Dharwar-Bellary-Mysore, Ranchi-Hazaribagh,
            Bastar, Sundergarh-Keonjhar, Aravalli belt.  [L10]
                 |
   STEP 3   PURANA               >> CUDDAPAH (crescent, concave to
            the coast) and VINDHYAN (the large north-central
            spread). Mark the GREAT UNCONFORMITY beneath. [L11]
                 |
   STEP 4   GONDWANA AND UPPER PALAEOZOIC >> FOUR BELTS: Damodar,
            Mahanadi, southern MP, Godavari troughs.      [L12]
                 |
   STEP 5   DECCAN AND RAJMAHAL FLOWS >> the north-western lava
            spread. Remember: the OUTLINE YOU DRAW IS THE RESIDUE,
            about half the original.                      [L12]
                 |
   STEP 6   TERTIARY             >> coastal Tertiary; Palaeocene;
            Lower and Middle Miocene; SHIWALIKS AND TIPAM SERIES
            along the Himalayan front.                    [L13]
                 |
   STEP 7   QUATERNARY           >> the Great Plain, the coastal
            strips, the Kachchh and Rajasthan surfaces.    [L13]
                 |
   STEP 8   LINEAMENTS (overlay, drawn last, in a second colour)
            >> GREAT BOUNDARY FAULT: Vindhyan System | Aravallis,
               about 800 km.
            >> MAIN CENTRAL THRUST: heads the fault list of the
               extra-Peninsula.
   ===================================================================
   !! A learner who can draw the build IN THAT ORDER has internalised
      the chronology. A learner who memorises the finished sixteen-class
      map has internalised a picture. The order IS the content.
```
